Boundary Compactified Imaginary Liouville Theory
Yang Xiao, Yuxiao Xie

Abstract
We generalize the construction of Compactified Imaginary Liouville Theory (CILT), a non-unitary logarithmic Conformal Field Theory (CFT) defined on closed surfaces, to surfaces with boundary. Starting from a compactified Gaussian Free Field (GFF) with Neumann boundary condition, we perturb it by adding in curvature terms and exponential potentials on both the bulk and the boundary. In physics, this theory is conjectured to describe the scaling limit of loop models such as the Potts and O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) models. To define it mathematically, the curvature terms require a detailed analysis of the topology, and the potential terms are defined using the imaginary Guassian Multiplicative Chaos (GMC). We prove that the resulting probabilistic path integral satisfies the axioms of CFT, including Segal’s gluing axioms. This work provides the foundation for future studies of boundary CILT and will also help with the understanding of CILT.



1
Introduction

As a special case of Quantum Field Theories (QFTs), Conformal Field Theory (CFT) is a general physical framework that is expected to characterize the small-scale behavior of QFTs. It also plays a central role in understanding critical phenomena in statistical physics, and serves as the building block in string theory, among other applications. Thanks to the rich conformal symmetry in dimension
2
, the groundbreaking work of Belavin–Polyakov–Zamolodchikov
[
10
]
successfully solved a class of CFTs (known as the minimal models) under the conformal bootstrap hypothesis. With the help of the Virasoro algebra and the Ward identities, they were able to express the correlation functions as sums of certain special functions (known as the conformal blocks) multiplied by the structure constants. Their work has since greatly inspired mathematicians and led to the application of CFT to modular forms, representation theories of infinite-dimensional Lie algebras and vertex algebras, monstrous moonshine, geometric Langlands theory, and knot theory, to mention a few. On the other hand, several rigorous formulations of CFT have been proposed in the mathematical community: the vertex algebra approach by Kac
[
40
]
grew out of the representation-theoretical structure of CFTs; Frenkel and Ben-Zvi
[
25
]
formalized CFTs in the context of algebraic geometry, and this was further expanded by Beilinson and Drinfeld
[
9
]
; the lecture notes
[
27
]
emphasized the probabilistic approach based on the Feynman path integral; Segal
[
69
]
proposed an axiomatic formulation inspired by the path integral approach and designed to capture the geometric nature of the conformal bootstrap.

Yet it remains challenging to construct a nontrivial CFT satisfying any given set of axioms.
It was not until recently that a mathematical construction of Liouville CFT was brought within reach by the probabilistic approach
[
17
,
33
]
.
This then led to great success in the mathematical study of this theory, opening the door to the first proof of the DOZZ formula for the structure constant
[
44
]
, Segal’s axioms
[
30
]
, the conformal bootstrap
[
31
]
, and many more results to come.

In addition to the standard Liouville theory, the physics community is also looking for an imaginary version of Liouville CFT that can be used to describe the scaling limit of loop models, a major class of models in statistical physics (see
[
39
]
for instance; for progress in the physics literature, we refer to the review
[
63
]
and references therein)
. More recently, a new CFT has been constructed and is expected to serve as a candidate: the
Compactified Imaginary Liouville Theory

[
32
]
, or
CILT
for short. Specifically, on a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
with conformal metric
g
, the
imaginary
Liouville action is defined by

S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\mu\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

where
v_{g}
is the volume,
K_{g}
is the scalar curvature, and the parameters are
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
,
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
Q=\beta/2-2/\beta
. Here the Liouville field
\phi
is a map from the surface
\Sigma
to the circle
{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
with radius
R>0
. Formally, the path integral of a test functional
F(\phi)
is

\int F(\phi)\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi,

where
{\mathrm{D}}\phi
is the formal “Lebesgue” measure on the infinite-dimensional space of maps from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
.
When
\mu=Q=0
, this reduces to the compactified Gaussian Free Field (GFF) (we refer to
[
27
, Lecture 1.4]
and
[
20
, Subsections 6.3.5 and 10.4.1]
for the physics viewpoint, and also
[
19
, Subsection 2.1.3]
for a mathematical description)
.
To date, it remains a challenge to give a mathematical construction of the
non-compactified
imaginary Liouville theory, which would get rid of the rationality conditions on the parameters.
A possible approach is proposed in
[
72
]
with simulations on the DOZZ formula and rigorous heuristics when
\Sigma
is a circle. Let us also mention another rigorous construction of the timelike Liouville theory
[
15
]
which produces the DOZZ formula and studies the semiclassical limits under neutrality conditions.

In contrast to the real Liouville CFT, the action of CILT is complex-valued, and the field is also compactified. These differences lead to completely new algebraic, geometric, and probabilistic phenomena in CILT. The CFT structure of CILT is also drastically distinct from the real theory. Specifically, the Hamiltonian (the infinitesimal generator of dilations) acting on the canonical Hilbert space is not self-adjoint, which means that CILT is a
non-unitary
CFT. One also finds that its spectrum is countably infinite (thus
non-rational
), and that the chiral and anti-chiral parts have different representations (thus
non-diagonal
). Moreover, the Hamiltonian is
not
diagonalizable, and it is possible to construct explicit Jordan blocks. This suggests that the correlation functions of CILT may have logarithmic singularities, indicating that CILT is a
logarithmic CFT
, an
active topic of research
in physics
[
22
,
23
,
35
,
45
,
50
,
54
,
56
,
58
,
64
,
67
,
14
]
and mathematics
[
5
,
46
,
49
]
.
We stress that results on the Hamiltonian and its spectrum have been obtained and will appear in a future work. The Jordan cell structure and the Virasoro representation are works in progress. The logarithmic behavior is observed at least when the correlation function involves a Jordan partner (see also
[
14
,
67
]
).

As demonstrated in
[
10
]
, under the assumption that the scaling limit of the discrete statistical physics model corresponds to a CFT, they can predict critical exponents of the model with the help of Virasoro algebra.
Thus physicists have been working on the classification of CFTs, and also discovering the CFT aspects in the scaling limits of all kinds of discrete models
[
10
,
12
]
.
Loop models, for example, have been tackled with the so-called Coulomb gas formalism (see
[
12
,
18
,
53
]
for instance). Roughly speaking, it maps a loop configuration to a height function by, for example, assigning orientations to each loop, and the height function is generally believed to be some variant of a GFF. In addition, the mapping between the loop and the height function eventually requires the field to be compactified. The Coulomb gas representation also introduces a background charge
Q
(see the curvature term in the action of CILT), which shifts the central charge 1 (the one for GFF) to
1-6Q^{2}
. Finally, as argued in
[
38
,
43
,
42
]
(and the review
[
66
]
, also some criticism in
[
29
]
), an imaginary Liouville potential should be also included in the action, hence physicists reach the path integral used for their studies of loop models.

In this paper, following and extending the work of
[
32
]
, we construct the
Boundary Compactified Imaginary Liouville Theory
(
BCILT
) and prove Segal’s axioms for it. As stressed in
[
13
]
, the study of CFTs with boundary is important in understanding the CFT structure and for describing critical phenomena in the presence of a conformal boundary. In the case of the real Liouville theory, the boundary CFT has been constructed
[
36
,
61
,
73
]
, its structure constants are computed
[
62
,
60
,
4
,
2
]
, and the conformal bootstrap program is initiated in
[
34
]
. This has been shown to help in the study of the theory without boundary. For example,
using the conformal bootstrap for the boundary Liouville theory,

[
28
]
proved the connection between the boundary three-point function and the fusion kernel as proposed in
[
57
]
. Similar results have been obtained in physics for rational CFTs
[
65
,
8
,
26
]
. Therefore, we expect that the same can be achieved for CILT, and our construction of BCILT lays the foundation for further studies in this direction.

Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface with boundary
\partial\Sigma
and
g
a conformal metric on
\Sigma
. We shall construct the CFT obtained by quantizing the imaginary Liouville action

S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\mu\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

(1)

where
Q,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
,
\mu_{\partial}:\partial\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a piecewise constant function. Here
K_{g}
,
v_{g}
,
k_{g}
,
\ell_{g}
denote the scalar curvature, volume, geodesic curvature, and length with respect to
g
, and the Liouville field
\phi:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
is a map from the surface to the circle with radius
R>0
. For a test functional
F(\phi)
, we would like to construct the path integral

\int F(\phi)\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi,

where
{\mathrm{D}}\phi
is the formal “Lebesgue” measure on the space of maps
\phi
from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
with Neumann boundary condition at
\partial\Sigma
. When
\mu=0
,
\mu_{\partial}=0
, this becomes the Coulomb gas formalism with boundary. Otherwise we fix
Q
by

Q=\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{2}{\beta}.

The well-definedness of the Liouville action requires that
\beta R,QR\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
. Because of the boundary, these conditions are slightly stronger than in CILT (where one requires only that
\beta R,QR\in\mathbb{Z}
). Nevertheless, they can be satisfied by choosing
R
appropriately, and we are still in the rational regime (
\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{1}{Q}\mathbb{Z}\not=\{0\}
) studied in
[
32
]
.

1.1
Sketch of the construction

The construction of the path integral begins with the observation that the first term
\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
in the Liouville action (
1
) is the action of a compactified Gaussian Free Field (GFF). Suppose
\phi
is a smooth map from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
, so that
d\phi
is a well-defined closed
1
-form with coefficients in
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
. By the Hodge decomposition, there exist a unique smooth function
f:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
with mean zero and a unique harmonic
1
-form
\omega
such that
d\phi=df+\omega
. Then
\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}|df|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\Sigma}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
. We interpret the path integral for the compactified GFF as integrating over the product measure

e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi:=e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|df|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}f\otimes e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]\otimes{\mathrm{d}}c,

where the first part is given by the standard GFF with mean zero and Neumann boundary condition at
\partial\Sigma
,
{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]
is the counting measure on the first cohomology group
H^{1}(\Sigma;2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}})
with coefficients in
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
{\mathrm{d}}c
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
. The compactified field
\phi
is then

\phi(z)=c+f(z)+\textstyle\int_{x_{0}}^{z}\omega,

sampled according to the measure above. Here we fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in\Sigma
and integrate over any path from
x_{0}
to
z
. Since
\phi
takes values in
\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}
, this is well-defined. The details of this construction are carried out in
Section

3
, see in particular
3.7
and
3.9
.

We interpret the other terms in the Liouville action as functionals on this measure space. One has to be careful since
\phi
is now multi-valued when viewed as a map to
{\mathbb{R}}
. As a first observation, for the Liouville action to be well-defined, these terms should be invariant under the translation
\phi\mapsto\phi+2\pi R
, which forces

\frac{QR}{4\pi}\left(\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+2\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right)\in\mathbb{Z},\qquad\text{and}\qquad e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta R\cdot 2\pi}=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}R\cdot 2\pi}=1.

By the Gauss–Bonnet formula, these conditions amount to
QR\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\beta R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
.

To make sense of the curvature terms
\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
and
\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}
, the idea is to integrate over a domain of full measure on which
\phi
is single-valued. This is done by cutting the surface along a family of curves called a separating family (defined in
Section

2.5
), leaving a null-homologous domain. With appropriate correction terms that depend on the cutting curves, the resulting integral can be shown to be invariant, except that changing the homotopy classes of the cutting curves introduces an integral anomaly, which is why we actually need
QR\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
for a general surface.
Section

5
is devoted to this.

Finally, the Liouville potential terms
\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
and
\int_{\partial\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}
have been studied as a probabilistic object in
[
47
,
48
]
, called the imaginary Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos (GMC). The parameter
\beta
is restricted to
(0,\sqrt{2})
, and important estimates of the bulk imaginary GMC
\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
(with Dirichlet boundaries actually, see Segal’s axioms below) are made in
[
32
]
for the construction of CILT. In Section
6
, we apply the techniques used in
[
32
]
to the terms
\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
and
\int_{\partial\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}
on surfaces with Neumann boundary.

As a concrete example, we specialize to the case of the disk. Let
g
be a Neumann extendible (
meaning that it extends to a symmetric metric on the doubled surface, see
Section

2.1
for the precise definition
) conformal metric on the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
. We fix a compactification radius
R>0
. As discussed above, we require
\beta\in(0,\sqrt{2})
,
QR\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\beta R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
. The first cohomology is trivial, so the curvature terms need no special treatment. Let
X_{g}
be the Neumann GFF on
(\mathbb{D},g)
. We define the bulk and boundary GMCs
M_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})
and
L_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})
by

M_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\beta^{2}/2}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta X_{g,\varepsilon}(x)}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x),\ \ \ L_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\beta^{2}/4}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta/2X_{g,\varepsilon}(x)}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x),

where
X_{g,\varepsilon}
is a suitable regularization of the random distribution
X_{g}
at scale
\varepsilon
with respect to the metric
g
. The convergence is nontrivial, and we refer to
[
47
]
for the details. Writing
\phi=c+X_{g}
, the path integral for a test functional
F(\phi)
is

\langle F\rangle_{\mathbb{D},g}:=C(g)\int_{\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\phi)\,e^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\mu M_{g}(\phi,\mathbb{D})-L_{g}(\phi,\mu_{\partial}1_{\partial\mathbb{D}})}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c,

where
C(g)
is an explicit constant depending only on the metric
g
and the expectation is over the GFF
X_{g}
.

Let us introduce an important type of functionals called
electric operators
. For
\alpha,\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\alpha R\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\eta R\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, we define formally the electric operators
V_{\alpha}(z)
,
V_{\eta}(x)
at
z\in\mathbb{D}
,
x\in\partial\mathbb{D}
as the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit of

V_{\alpha,g,\varepsilon}(z):=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha\phi_{g,\varepsilon}(z)},V_{\eta,g,\varepsilon}(x):=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta}{2}\phi_{g,\varepsilon}(x)},

where
\phi_{g,\varepsilon}=c+X_{g,\varepsilon}
, and the regularization is necessary since
\phi
does not make sense pointwise. We define the path integral with electric insertions as

\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\mathbb{D},g}:=C\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\phi)\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi)\,e^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\mu M_{g}(\phi,\mathbb{D})-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi,\mu_{\partial}1_{\partial\mathbb{D}})}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c,

where
{\mathbf{z}}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{\mathfrak{s}})
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{\mathfrak{s}})
are the data of bulk insertions, and similarly for the boundary data
{\mathbf{x}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, and
V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi)=\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})
. Here the constant
C
depends on
(g,{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{x}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}})
. Since
V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi)
should be invariant under the translation
\phi\mapsto\phi+2\pi R
, we require that
\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{j}R+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}R\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The existence of this limit is proved in Section
7.1
, which relies on the imaginary Cameron–Martin theorem for the GMC (Proposition
6.4
). It turns out that we should assume
\alpha_{j},\eta_{j}>Q
for all
j
to guarantee convergence.

Taking
F=1
, we obtain the
correlation functions

\langle V_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}({\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\mathbb{D},g}
, which are fundamental objects exhibiting rich symmetries.
These symmetries, especially Ward identities to be proven (the proof will be in a future work), are essential tools in the bootstrap program demonstrated in
[
10
]
. The three-point function on the sphere (which is the structure constant of CILT) is known to be given by the imaginary DOZZ formula
[
32
]
. In Section
8
, we show that the correlation functions on the disk (which contain the structure constants of BCILT) involve combinations of Dotsenko–Fateev and Selberg integrals. More precisely, they are expectations of mixed moments of the boundary and bulk GMCs. In particular, when the bulk
\mu=0
, the boundary one-point function has a formula which produces the Fyodorov–Bouchaud formula. Note that there have been several papers on moments of the real boundary GMC
[
62
,
59
,
60
]
(built with tools like the BPZ equations), and we expect similar results for the imaginary one. It would also be interesting to connect our integrals with the Coulomb gas integrals in
[
49
]
.

Apart from electric operators, we can also introduce another type called
magnetic operators
. This is when we require the field
\phi
to have a winding number
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(called the magnetic charge) around an interior point
z\in\Sigma^{\circ}=\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma
. As before, we put magnetic charges
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{\mathfrak{s}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
at
{\mathbf{z}}
. In order to make sense of the electric operators
e^{\alpha_{j}\phi(z_{j})}
(even for an otherwise smooth field), we specify a nonzero tangent vector
v_{j}
at
z_{j}
and define
\phi(z_{j})
to be the limit of
\phi(w)
as
w
approaches
z_{j}
in the direction of
v_{j}
. Then the above construction carries over, where
\omega
is now a closed
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
with the prescribed cycles around each
z_{j}
. The combined operators are called the
electromagnetic operators
.
Note that we do not put magnetic operators on the boundary, so electromagnetic operators exist only in the interior
\Sigma^{\circ}
. They also produce certain types of Coulomb gas integrals, for which we refer to
[
32
, Section 7]
and
[
52
]
for more explanations.

As a concrete example, let us consider the case of an annulus
\mathbb{A}
with two magnetic insertions
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},m_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
at
{\mathbf{z}}=(z_{1},z_{2})\in({\mathbb{A}}^{\circ})^{2}
. See
Fig.

1
for an illustration. In the notation of Section
2.6
, the set of possible choices of
\omega
is in bijection with the affine subspace
H^{1}_{{\mathbf{m}}}(\mathbb{A}\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
of
H^{1}(\mathbb{A}\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
consisting of cohomology classes with the prescribed cycles
{\mathbf{m}}
around
{\mathbf{z}}
. We have
\int_{\partial_{+}{\mathbb{A}}}\omega=\int_{\partial_{-}{\mathbb{A}}}\omega+m_{1}+m_{2}
, where
\partial_{\pm}{\mathbb{A}}
are the outer and inner boundaries of
{\mathbb{A}}
, both with counterclockwise orientation. Then the cohomology class of
\omega
is determined by choosing
\int_{\partial_{-}{\mathbb{A}}}\omega
(or
\int_{\partial_{+}{\mathbb{A}}}\omega
), so that
H^{1}_{{\mathbf{m}}}(\mathbb{A}\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the measure
e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]
becomes a sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. To make sense of the curvature terms, the separating family should include curves connecting the insertions with the boundary. Changing this family as in
Fig.

1
introduces an anomaly of
2\pi
by a Gauss–Bonnet calculation.
We define similarly the electromagnetic operators as the limit of
V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi)
, where
\phi
now has the multi-valued term
\int_{x_{0}}^{z}\omega
.
Then the path integral
\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}
on
\Sigma
is

C\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sum_{[\omega]\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})}e^{-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}/2\pi R\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\phi)\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi)\,e^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\mu M_{g}(\phi,\Sigma)-L_{g}(\phi,\mu_{\partial}1_{\partial\Sigma})}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c,

where
\phi=c+X_{g}+2\pi R\int_{x_{0}}^{z}\omega
(
\omega
has coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
) and
C
depends on
(g,{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbf{x}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{v}})
.

(a)

left

(b)

right

Figure 1
:
On the left is a possible choice of a separating family
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})
on the annulus
\mathbb{A}
with two Neumann boundary circles
\partial_{\pm}{\mathbb{A}}
and two insertions
(z_{1},z_{2})
. Note that the orientation of the
d_{j}
does not matter. On the right, we obtain a new separating family by replacing
d_{1}
with the blue curve between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. Then the value of
\int_{x_{0}}^{z}\omega
changes by
m_{1}
, the winding number around
z_{1}
.

As proposed in
[
69
]
, an important step in solving the conformal bootstrap of a CFT is constructing the
Segal functor
. This construction associates to a surface with Dirichlet boundary an operator, called the
amplitude
, whose integral kernel is given by the path integral over fields with prescribed boundary values at the Dirichlet boundary. Then one can recover the correlation functions by gluing such surfaces along the Dirichlet boundary, which corresponds to composing the amplitude operators. In boundary CFTs, the additional complexity is that one can have both the Neumann boundary and the Dirichlet boundary, and they create corners at the intersection. We refer to
[
30
,
34
,
32
]
for previous works in this direction for the real Liouville CFT and CILT without boundary. For BCILT, the topological structure further complicates the problem, as already seen in defining the correlation functions. One of the main contributions of this paper is a clear framework that facilitates such generalizations.

1.2
Statement of the main results

In order to describe the general situation in a precise manner, we propose the following definition:

Definition 1.1
.

An
extended surface
consists of the following data:

(1)

\Sigma
is a compact Riemann surface with corners (see, e.g.,
[
34
, Definition 2.2]
) of genus
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(2)

We put labels on the boundary circles of
\Sigma
so that we have a partition
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma
where:

•

\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma
consists of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}
real-analytic circles
c_{1}^{\mathrm{N}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}}^{\mathrm{N}}
with Neumann boundary condition.

•

\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
consists of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}
real-analytic circles
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}}
with Dirichlet boundary condition.

•

\partial_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma
consists of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}
piecewise real-analytic circles
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}}^{\mathrm{M}}
with mixed boundary condition. More precisely, each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M}}
consists of
k_{i}
real-analytic semicircles
c_{i,1}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}},\ldots,c_{i,k_{i}}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}
with Neumann boundary condition and
k_{i}
real-analytic semicircles
c_{i,1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,c_{i,k_{i}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
with Dirichlet boundary condition. We assume that
c_{i,j}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}\cap c_{i,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}=c_{i,j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cap c_{i,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}=\varnothing
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
and that the intersections
c_{i,j}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}\cap c_{i,j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
are orthogonal (if nonempty), i.e., the corners of
\Sigma
are right angles.

The precise meaning of the labels will become evident shortly. We write
\partial_{\mathrm{M_{N}}}\Sigma=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}}\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k_{i}}c_{i,j}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}
,
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{\mathrm{M_{N}}}\Sigma
, and likewise
\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
,
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. In other words,
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
is the full Neumann boundary, whereas
\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma
contains only the Neuamnn circles, and similarly for
{\mathrm{D}}
.

(3)

{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,\zeta_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}},\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,\zeta_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})
where:

•

\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,\zeta_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}}
are real-analytic parametrizations
{\mathbb{T}}=\{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}:\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\to\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
of the Dirichlet circles
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}}
in
\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
.

•

\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,\zeta_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
are Neumann extendible (see
Section

2.1
) real-analytic parametrizations
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}=\{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}:\theta\in[0,\pi]\}\to\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
of the Dirichlet semicircles
c_{1,1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}},k_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
in
\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
, where
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}}k_{i}
. We relabel the indices of these semicircles accordingly as
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
(note that a boundary component may contain several Dirichlet semicircles).

A Dirichlet circle or semicircle is
outgoing
(resp.
incoming
) if the orientation induced by its parametrization coincides with (resp. is reverse to) the boundary orientation induced by
\Sigma

(we use the convention that the boundary of the unit disk has counterclockwise orientation)
. We write
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}=1
(resp.
-1
) if
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
is outgoing (resp. incoming), and likewise
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
for
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
, so that
{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}=(\varsigma_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,\varsigma_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}},\varsigma_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,\varsigma_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})
encodes the orientations of the parametrizations in
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
.

To simplify the notation later on, we write
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathbb{T}}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
, so that
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
can be viewed as a diffeomorphism
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\xrightarrow{\sim}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. We maintain this distinction to emphasize the fact that the Dirichlet boundary is parametrized.

(4)

{\mathbf{z}}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{\mathfrak{s}})
are distinct points on
\Sigma^{\circ}=\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma
.

(5)

{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathfrak{t}})
are distinct points on
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
.

Such an extended surface is said to be of type
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}},{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
. See
Fig.

2
for an illustration.

Figure 2
:
An extended surface of type
(1,1,2,1,2,2,2)
and its double

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface and
g
a Neumann extendible (see
Section

2.1
) conformal metric on
\Sigma
. Fix a compactification radius
R>0
. We shall give a probabilistic
definition of the formal path integral (see
7.1
for the case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
7.4
for the general case)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})=\int F(\phi)\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}\phi(v_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\phi(x_{j})}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi,

where the integral is over maps
\phi:\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying the mixed boundary condition

\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0,\qquad{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}},

and having a given winding number
m_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
around each
z_{j}\in\Sigma^{\circ}
. Here
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}:{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
is the Dirichlet boundary value
(
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
encodes the winding numbers on the Dirichlet circles, see
Section

3.2
),
F
is in a suitable function space containing the constants,
S_{\mathrm{L}}
is the imaginary Liouville functional

S_{\mathrm{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\mu\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}\phi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

and the parameters are:

•

\beta\in(0,\sqrt{2})
such that
\beta R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
(or
{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\mu_{\partial}=0
).

•

Q=\beta/2-2/\beta
such that
QR\in 4{\mathbb{Z}}
(or
2{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\Sigma
has no corners). It is called the
background charge
.

•

\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mu_{\partial}:{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}}
is piecewise constant. They are called the
cosmological constants
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\mathfrak{s}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\mathfrak{t}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{t}}
such that

\begin{cases}\forall\,j,\,\alpha_{j},\eta_{j}>Q,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{j}R+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}R\in{\mathbb{Z}}.\end{cases}

They are called the
electric charges
.

•

{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{\mathfrak{s}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. They are called the
magnetic charges
.

•

{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\mathfrak{s}})
where
v_{j}
is a nonzero tangent vector at
z_{j}
.

We remark that both the Neumann boundary
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
and the Dirichlet boundary
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
can be empty. The case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
is exactly CILT as studied in
[
32
]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

This defines a Conformal Field Theory in the following sense:

(1)

(
Convergence
)
The path integral
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})
defines an
L^{2}
function in the boundary value
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}

with respect to an appropriate measure (defined using the GFF on the (semi)circle, see
Section

3.2
).
We view it as the Schwartz kernel of an operator
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)
acting on the
L^{2}
space on the measure space of boundary values
, called the
amplitude
. In particular, if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, this defines a complex number, called the
correlation function
.

(2)

(
Weyl anomaly
)
This theory is conformally covariant in the following sense. For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible (see
Section

2.1
) with
\rho|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\big(F\big(\cdot-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\exp\left(\frac{c_{\mathrm{L}}}{96\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\big(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\Delta_{\alpha_{j},m_{j}}\rho(z_{j})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\Delta_{\eta_{j}}\rho(x_{j})\right),

where

c_{\mathrm{L}}=1-6Q^{2}

is the
central charge
of the theory and

\Delta_{\alpha,m}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}-Q\right)+\frac{m^{2}R^{2}}{4},\qquad\Delta_{\eta}=\frac{\eta}{2}\left(\frac{\eta}{2}-Q\right)

are the
conformal dimensions
of the vertex operators
V_{\alpha,m}(v)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha\phi(v)}
,
V_{\eta}(x)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta}{2}\phi(x)}
, respectively.

(3)

(
Spin
)
For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, we have

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)={\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}R\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha_{j}-Q)m_{j}\theta_{j}},

where
e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}}=(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{1}}v_{1},\ldots,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{\mathfrak{s}}}v_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

(4)

(
Gluing
)
This defines a projective functor from the category where morphisms are extended surfaces and composition is given by gluing, to the category of Hilbert spaces. For the precise statement, see
7.8
.

The novelty of this work consists mainly of the treatment of the curvature terms in the presence of boundary with possible corners (see
Section

5
) and the corresponding estimates for the GMC required for proving convergence (see
Section

6
). In particular, we generalize several estimates in
[
32
]
, like the exponential moments of the GMC and its Cameron–Martin theorem, to surfaces with Neumann boundary.

1.3
Open questions and future works

For more insights on this CFT, we refer to
[
32
, Section 1]
. As another concrete example of a CFT, it would be interesting to understand the relation between BCILT and other
important
continuum models like the Schramm–Loewner Evolution
[
68
]
and Conformal Loop Ensembles
[
70
]
. They are
also powerful ingredients in
the mating-of-trees formalism
[
20
]
and are expected to have strong interplay with CFTs (first advocated in physics like
[
37
]
or the review
[
66
]
). In the case of Liouville CFT, the connection between these models has led to mutual better understandings like
[
7
,
2
,
1
]
, and also unexpected results for statistical physics models like
[
3
,
55
]
.

There are several questions to be answered for BCILT itself. First, it is unclear how (or whether it is meaningful) to put magnetic charges on the (Neumann) boundary, since having a nontrivial winding number violates the Neumann boundary condition. Second, the structure constants of BCILT are not fully understood yet. In Section
8
, we briefly describe how these constants can be written as certain Coulomb gas integrals, and we hope their explicit forms will be studied somewhere else. The next step would be to investigate the conformal bootstrap for BCILT, which might be easier than the bulk case, see
[
41
,
58
]
for similar works in this direction.

1.4
Organization of the paper

The paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
is a compilation of the geometric and topological preliminaries used in this paper.
Section

3
defines the spaces of random fields in the path integrals.
Section

4
verifies gluing for the free theory, i.e., the compactified free boson with boundary.
Section

5
is devoted to the curvature terms in the Liouville action.
Section

6
provides all GMC estimates used in the construction of BCILT. Finally, in
Section

7
, we prove
1.2
. For the previous sections, the reader who is only interested in the correlation functions (as opposed to Segal’s amplitudes) can assume
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and skip
Sections

2.4
,
2.7
,
3.2
,
3.4
and
4
.

also acknowledges the support of ANR-21-CE40-0003. Y. Xie acknowledges support from the Jean-Pierre Aguilar scholarship from the Fondation CFM pour la Recherche. Both authors acknowledge the support from the Simons foundation grant “Probabilistic Paths to QFT”.

2
Geometric preliminaries

In this section, we collect the geometric and topological ingredients needed for BCILT.

2.1
Neumann doubling

A useful method for studying the Neumann boundary condition is
Cardy’s doubling trick
.

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. The
(Neumann) double
of
\Sigma
is the surface obtained by gluing
\Sigma
and its oppositely oriented copy
\overline{\Sigma}
along their Neumann boundaries via the identity map
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\xleftrightarrow{\sim}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\overline{\Sigma}
. We denote it by
\Sigma^{\#2}
. It is naturally an extended surface with
\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma^{\#2}=\partial_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma^{\#2}=\varnothing
,
\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma^{\#2}={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\cup{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\overline{\Sigma}
. If
\Sigma
is of type
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}},{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
, then
\Sigma^{\#2}
is of type
(2{\mathfrak{g}},0,2{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}},0,0,2{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
. See
Fig.

2
for an illustration.

Consider the
reflection

\tau
on
\Sigma^{\#2}
with respect to
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\overline{\Sigma}
, which is an antiholomorphic involution exchanging
\Sigma
and
\overline{\Sigma}
. A functorial object
u
on
\Sigma^{\#2}
is
even
(resp.
odd
) if
\tau_{*}u=u
(resp.
\tau_{*}u=-u
). This definition applies to functions, tensors, distributions, etc.

Any tensor
u
on
\Sigma
has a unique even extension to
\Sigma^{\#2}
, denoted by
u^{\#2}
. We say
u
is
Neumann extendible
if
u^{\#2}
is smooth at
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
. We are interested in the following cases:

•

If a metric
g
is Neumann extendible, then
\tau
is an isometry with respect to
g^{\#2}
, and
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
is necessarily geodesic, being its fixed point set.

•

If a function
f
is Neumann extendible, then
f
satisfies the Neumann boundary condition
\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
.

•

If a
1
-form
\omega
is Neumann extendible, then
i_{\nu}\omega|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
, where
i_{\nu}
denotes contraction with
\nu
.

One can view
{\mathbb{T}}
as the double of
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
with respect to
\partial{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
. A real-analytic embedding
\zeta:{\mathbb{T}}_{+}\to\Sigma
with
\zeta(\partial{\mathbb{T}}_{+})\subset{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
is
Neumann extendible
if its unique extension
\zeta^{\#2}:{\mathbb{T}}\to\Sigma^{\#2}
with
\zeta^{\#2}(e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\theta})=\tau(\zeta^{\#2}(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}))
is real-analytic. In
1.1
, we require the parametrizations
\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
to be Neumann extendible, so that the corresponding parametrizations
(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{\#2}
for
\Sigma^{\#2}
are real-analytic.

In view of the relation between the Neumann boundary condition and Neumann extendibility, one can think of a boundary CFT on an extended surface
\Sigma
with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
as an ordinary CFT on its double
\Sigma^{\#2}
where the path integrals are restricted to even fields. This doubling idea will be a guiding principle throughout the paper.

2.2
Distribution spaces

For important technical reasons (see
Section

3.1
), it is necessary to integrate over fields that are not even in
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, i.e., they will generally be distributions. Therefore, we need to specify the appropriate distribution space on an extended surface. As a rule of thumb, we require the space
{\mathcal{D}}
of test functions to be Neumann extendible and supported away from the Dirichlet boundary, and we define the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
of distributions to be the subspace of even distributions on the double.

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, we define
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
to be the usual distribution space, i.e., the space of continuous linear functionals on the space of compactly supported smooth
2
-forms on
\Sigma
. In general, we define
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
to be the space of even distributions on
\Sigma^{\#2}
. We view
C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
as a subspace of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
in the following way. For
u\in C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we view it as a distribution in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with

\langle u,\eta\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma^{\#2}}u^{\#2}\eta

for a test
2
-form
\eta
on
\Sigma^{\#2}
. Let

{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}):f\text{ Neumann extendible},\operatorname{supp}f\cap{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing\}.

Note that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}):\operatorname{supp}f\subset\Sigma^{\circ}\}\subset{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, with equality if and only if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
. The pairing between
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
depends on a volume form. Let
g
be a Neumann extendible metric on
\Sigma
. Then its volume form
{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
is also Neumann extendible. For
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,
f\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we define

\int_{\Sigma}uf\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\langle u,(f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})^{\#2}\rangle.

This is consistent with the usual meaning of the integral for
u\in C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. Note that the constant functions are in
{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
if and only if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
. In this case, we define

{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}):\textstyle\int_{\Sigma}u\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=0\},

which depends implicitly on
g
. We have a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. The constant part is called the
zero mode
.

For the boundary value, we also need distribution spaces on
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
. The definitions are similar, except that we use the canonical angle form
d\theta
to identify
1
-forms with functions. We define
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})=C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
to be the space of continuous linear functionals on the space of smooth functions on
{\mathbb{T}}
. Viewing
{\mathbb{T}}
as the double of
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
, we define
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})=\{f\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}}):f\text{ Neumann extendible}\}\supsetneq C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
to be the space of even distributions on
{\mathbb{T}}
, i.e., distributions
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
such that
\langle u,f(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta})\rangle=\langle u,f(e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\theta})\rangle
for
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
. We define
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{0}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
as above, so that we have decompositions
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
. For
\widetilde{\varphi}
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
or
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
, we denote its decomposition by

\widetilde{\varphi}=c+\varphi,\qquad\text{where }\textstyle c=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{\varphi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta,\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta=0,

i.e., the presence or absence of the tilde indicates the presence or absence of the zero mode. Finally, for an extended surface
\Sigma
, we define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}.

This is consistent with the natural identification
{\mathcal{F}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)={\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{T}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbb{T}}_{+})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}
for any function class
{\mathcal{F}}
(
{\mathcal{D}}
,
C^{k}
,
L^{p}
, etc.), and it is merely for notational convenience. We have a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, and for
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}^{\mathrm{D}},\ldots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{\mathrm{D}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}},\ldots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we denote its decomposition by
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}={\mathbf{c}}+{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, with the obvious meanings.

2.3
Laplacians and Green’s functions

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface and
g
a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
. We denote by
\Delta_{g}=d^{*}d=-{*}d{*}d
the geometer’s Laplacian with respect to
g
, acting on functions on
\Sigma
. It extends to a continuous map
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
and its restriction to
{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
extends uniquely to an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with compact resolvent. By integration by parts, for
\phi\in C^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
satisfying the mixed boundary condition
\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
, we have

\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}\phi\,\Delta_{g}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}.

For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible,
1
1

1

This is to ensure that
e^{\rho}g
is also Neumann extendible.

\Delta_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{-\rho}\Delta_{g},\qquad{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},\qquad|d\phi|_{e^{\rho}g}^{2}=e^{-\rho}|d\phi|_{g}^{2},

so both sides are
conformally invariant
, i.e., independent of the choice of the conformal metric
g
.

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, then
\ker\Delta_{g}={\mathbb{R}}
and
\Delta_{g}:{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
is invertible. The
Green’s function

G_{g}
with respect to
g
is the Schwartz kernel (with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
) of this inverse. In other words, for
f\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,

(R_{g}f)(x)=\int_{\Sigma}G_{g}(x,x^{\prime})f(x^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x^{\prime})

solves

\Delta_{g}R_{g}f=f-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

with the Neumann boundary condition
\partial_{\nu}R_{g}f|_{\partial\Sigma}=0
(note that
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
). If
\partial\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we have

G_{g}(x,x^{\prime})=G_{g^{\#2}}(x,x^{\prime})+G_{g^{\#2}}(x,\tau(x^{\prime}))=G_{g^{\#2}}(x,x^{\prime})+G_{g^{\#2}}(\tau(x),x^{\prime}).

For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible,

G_{e^{\rho}g}(x,x^{\prime})=G_{g}(x,x^{\prime})-f(x)-f(x^{\prime})+\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g},

where

f(x)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}G_{g}(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}(y).

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, then
\ker\Delta_{g}=0
and
\Delta_{g}:{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
is invertible. The Green’s function
G_{g}
in this case is the Schwartz kernel of
\Delta_{g}^{-1}
. This is similar to but simpler than the previous case. There is no zero mode due to the Dirichlet boundary condition, and
G_{g}
is conformally invariant, so we sometimes also write
G_{\Sigma}
.

Remark
.

These facts are standard for manifolds without corners (see
[
71
, Chapter 5]
for example). For extended surfaces, one can reduce to this case by doubling, noting that
\Delta_{g}
is essentially the restriction of
\Delta_{g^{\#2}}
on
\Sigma^{\#2}
to even functions. The same remark applies to the next subsection.

2.4
Poisson and Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
.

Any
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in C({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
admits a unique harmonic extension
P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
to
\Sigma
with mixed boundary condition. More precisely,
P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\cap C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,
d{*}dP_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}=0
on
\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
,
\partial_{\nu}P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
,
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we have
(P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}})^{\#2}=P_{\Sigma^{\#2}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\#2}
. This defines the
Poisson operator

P_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
. It extends to a continuous linear map
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\cap C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with Schwartz kernel (with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}\theta
) in
C^{\infty}((\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)\times{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
given by
K_{P_{\Sigma}}(x,\cdot)=-{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\partial_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}G_{\Sigma}(x,\cdot)|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
for
x\in\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, where
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is the outward-pointing normal vector along
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
determined by
i_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{*}d\theta)=0
,
i_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(*{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{*}d\theta)=-{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}
. Note that the Hodge star
*
on
1
-forms is conformally invariant, so neither
P_{\Sigma}
nor
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
depends on a conformal metric.

For a general
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,
P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
is singular near
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, but the Dirichlet boundary condition
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
can be interpreted in the following weak sense. We extend the parametrizations in
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
uniquely to holomorphic or antiholomorphic parametrizations of a tubular neighborhood. Let
c
be a Dirichlet (semi)circle in
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
with parametrization
\zeta
. For
\phi\in C(\Sigma\setminus{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we define the
trace

\zeta^{*}\phi|_{c}
of
\phi
at
c
to be the limit of
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}\mapsto\phi(\zeta(e^{-t+{\mathrm{i}}\theta}))
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
or
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
as
t\to 0
, if it exists. Since
P_{\Sigma}
is continuous as a map
{\mathcal{D}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, it is easy to check that for any
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, the trace of
P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
at
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
exists and
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
in this sense.

It is well-known that
P_{\Sigma}
extends to a continuous linear map
H^{s}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
for
s\geq-1/2
, where
H^{s}
denotes the
L^{2}
Sobolev space of order
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
(see
[
71
, Chapter 5, Proposition 1.8]
).

The
Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator

{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
is defined by
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\partial_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}P_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
for
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, where
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is as above. Its Schwartz kernel
K_{{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}}(x,x^{\prime})={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{x}^{*}\partial_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}^{x}K_{P_{\Sigma}}(x,x^{\prime})|_{x\in{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=-{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}_{x}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}_{x^{\prime}}\partial_{\nu_{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}}^{x}\partial_{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}^{x^{\prime}}G_{\Sigma}(x,x^{\prime})|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\times{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
is singular at the diagonal. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we have
({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}})^{\#2}={\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#2}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\#2}
. By integration by parts, for
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\in C^{1}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\int_{\Sigma}|dP_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta,

where
g
is any conformal metric on
\Sigma
. We denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the
L^{2}
inner product on
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}\theta
, so that the right-hand side is
\langle{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\rangle
.

Remark
.

Our definition differs from that of
[
30
,
32
]
in that we use
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
to fix the length of the normal vector
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
along
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, whereas in
[
30
,
32
]
, the length is fixed by requiring it to be unit with respect to a conformal metric
g
. These two definitions coincide if
g
is
admissible
in the terminology there, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}g=|dz|^{2}/|z|^{2}
in a neighborhood of
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. In general, they are conformally equivalent and induce the same
L^{2}
inner product on
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
(in their version, the measure on
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
is induced by
g
).

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be the Fourier multiplier by
(|n|)_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
, i.e.,
{\mathbf{D}}1=0
,
{\mathbf{D}}\cos n\theta=|n|\cos n\theta
,
{\mathbf{D}}\sin n\theta=|n|\sin n\theta
for
n\neq 0
. It coincides with the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators on the unit disk
{\mathbb{D}}=\{z:|z|\leq 1\}
with Dirichlet boundary
{\mathbb{T}}
and the unit half disk
{\mathbb{D}}_{+}=\{z\in{\mathbb{D}}:\operatorname{im}z\geq 0\}
with Dirichlet boundary
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
and Neumann boundary
{\mathbb{D}}_{+}\cap{\mathbb{R}}=[-1,1]
. We use the same symbol
{\mathbf{D}}
to denote the corresponding product operator on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. Since the Green’s function on any Riemann surface has the same
\log
singularity at the diagonal, it is not hard to see that for any extended surface
\Sigma
, the difference
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}}
is a smoothing operator, i.e., its Schwartz kernel is in
C^{\infty}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\times{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
(see
[
34
, Lemma 5.7]
for a detailed proof).

2.5
Separating families

In preparation for
Section

5
, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. We relabel the boundary circles
c_{i}^{\mathrm{N}},c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}},c_{i}^{\mathrm{M}}
of
\Sigma
as
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\mathfrak{b}}
where
{\mathfrak{b}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Fix
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\mathfrak{s}})
where
v_{i}
is a nonzero tangent vector at
z_{i}
. A
separating family
of
\Sigma
with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
is a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=(a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{\mathfrak{g}},b_{\mathfrak{g}},d_{1},\ldots,d_{{\mathfrak{b}}+{\mathfrak{s}}-1})
where:

(1)

a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{\mathfrak{g}},b_{\mathfrak{g}}
are smoothly embedded circles (i.e., simple closed curves) on
\Sigma^{\circ}
.

(2)

d_{1},\ldots,d_{{\mathfrak{b}}+{\mathfrak{s}}-1}
are smoothly embedded semicircles (i.e., simple paths) on
\Sigma
such that for each
d_{i}
:

•

d_{i}^{\circ}\subset\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
,
\partial d_{i}\subset(\partial\Sigma\setminus(\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma\cap\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma))\cup{\mathbf{z}}
(so that
d_{i}
is disjoint from the corners of
\Sigma
).
2
2

2

By abuse of notation, we identify an (ordered) tuple with the union of its elements. For example, here
{\mathbf{z}}
means
\bigcup_{i}\{z_{i}\}
.

•

If
d_{i}\cap\partial\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, then
d_{i}
intersects
\partial\Sigma
orthogonally.

•

If
d_{i}\cap z_{j}\neq\varnothing
for some
j
, i.e.,
z_{j}\in\partial d_{i}
, then
v_{j}
is tangent to
d_{i}
at
z_{j}
.

Note that only the directions of the
v_{i}
are relevant.

(3)

Each
a_{i}
intersects
b_{i}
exactly once and the intersection is transversal and positively oriented. There are no other intersections between the curves in
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
, except for perhaps intersections between the
d_{i}
at their ends.

(4)

\partial\Sigma\cup{\mathbf{z}}\cup\bigcup_{i}d_{i}
is connected. Equivalently, if we view
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\mathfrak{b}},z_{1},\ldots,z_{\mathfrak{s}}
as vertices and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{{\mathfrak{b}}+{\mathfrak{s}}-1}
as edges between them, then they form a tree. In particular,
{\mathbf{z}}\subset\bigcup_{i}d_{i}
.

We write
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
3
3

3

We use the Cartesian product notation
\times
instead of the union notation
\cup
to emphasize that these are ordered tuples.
where
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(a_{i},b_{i})_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{g}}
is an
interior topological basis
of
\Sigma
and
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}+{\mathfrak{s}}-1}
. Clearly any interior topological basis extends to a separating family.

Remark
.

This generalizes the following definitions in
[
32
]
:

•

A
canonical geometric basis for
H_{1}(\Sigma)
is an interior topological basis.

•

A
canonical geometric basis for
H_{1}(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)
is a separating family in the case
({\mathfrak{g}},0,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},0,0,0,0)
.

•

A
defect graph
is a separating family in the case
({\mathfrak{g}},0,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},0,0,{\mathfrak{s}},0)
.

There is one subtle difference: in
[
32
]
, the tangent vector of a
d_{i}
at an end depends on whether
d_{i}
goes
from
or
to
that end, whereas in our definition, the orientation of
d_{i}
is irrelevant. The latter appears to be more natural, for reasons explained later.

Figure 3
:
A separating family of an extended surface

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
.

(1)

\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is connected.

(2)

Any simple circle on
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
bounds a compact region contained in
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
.

(3)

For any simple semicircle
d
on
\Sigma
with
\partial d\subset\partial\Sigma\cup{\mathbf{z}}
,
d^{\circ}\subset\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
, the set
\Sigma\setminus({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup d)
consists of two connected components.

Proof.

Consider the compact surface
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
obtained by cutting
\Sigma
along
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
. More precisely, we compactify the open surface
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
into a compact topological surface with boundary
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
using charts near
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
on
\Sigma
. Clearly
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is homeomorphic to the sphere minus
{\mathfrak{g}}+1
disjoint disks, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
boundary circles correspond to the
a_{i},b_{i}
and the last boundary circle corresponds to
\partial\Sigma\cup\bigcup_{i}d_{i}
. Then (1-2) are trivial. As for (3),
d
corresponds to a semicircle on
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
with ends in the last boundary circle, so it is clear that
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup d)\cong\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\circ}\setminus d
consists of two connected components.
∎

2.6
Cohomology

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. In BCILT, the Liouville field is a map
\phi:\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since the target space
{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
has nontrivial topology, the topology of
\Sigma
comes into play. In particular, we are interested in the first cohomology groups
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
,
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)
, and the kernel of the natural homomorphism
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)\to H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, which we denote by
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
.
4
4

4

Unless otherwise noted, homology and cohomology groups take coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Cohomology classes in these cohomology groups can be represented by differential forms:

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbb{R}})=\{\omega:d\omega=0,i_{\nu}\omega|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0\}/\{df:\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0\},

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma;{\mathbb{R}})=\{\omega:d\omega=0,i_{\nu}\omega|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0,{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\omega|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0\}/\{df:\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbb{R}})=\{df:\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0,f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}\text{ locally constant}\}/\{df:\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0\},

where
\omega
is a smooth
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
,
f
is a smooth function on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. Restricting the coefficients to
{\mathbb{Z}}
amounts to requiring that
\int_{c}\omega\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for any circle
c
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
and that
f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
takes values in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. To see that these quotients are isomorphic to the standard cohomology groups (e.g., singular cohomology), it suffices to check that the pairing
([\omega],[c])\mapsto\int_{c}\omega
induces isomorphisms with the duals of the corresponding homology groups. Since we shall make no use of this fact, we leave the proof to the interested reader. In particular,
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{2{\mathfrak{g}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}+{\mathfrak{s}}-1}
,
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}-1}
.

For each
z_{i}
, let
c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}
be the boundary of a small disk
D_{i}
on
\Sigma
centered at
z_{i}
, with the boundary orientation induced by
D_{i}
. The isotopy class of
c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}
does not depend on the choice of
D_{i}
. For
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{\mathfrak{s}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, we add the subscript
m
to a cohomology group
H^{1}(-)
to denote the preimage of
{\mathbf{m}}
under the homomorphism
H^{1}(-)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}},[\omega]\mapsto(\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega)_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Similarly, for
{\mathbf{k}}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
, we add the subscript
k
to
H^{1}(-)
to denote the preimage of
{\mathbf{k}}
under the homomorphism
H^{1}(-)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}},[\omega]\to(\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}\int_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}}\omega)_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
. Note that the orientations of the parametrizations
\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
are used here. For example,
H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
is the set of cohomology classes
[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
with
\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega=m_{i}
,
\int_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}}\omega=\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}k_{i}
. It is easy to see that
H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})=\varnothing
if and only if
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}=0
(i.e.,
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
) and
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}\neq\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}k_{i}
. If it is nonempty, it is affine over
H_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\cong H_{\mathbf{0}}^{1}(\Sigma)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{2{\mathfrak{g}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}-1}
in the sense that
H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})=[\omega]+H_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
for any
[\omega]\in H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
.

Concerning the group
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, we have an explicit isomorphism
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}/{\mathbb{Z}},[df]\mapsto(f|_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}})_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times(f|_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}})_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}+{\mathbb{Z}}
. This is essentially the inverse of the connecting homomorphism
H^{0}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)\to H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)
in the long exact sequence of cohomology for the pair
(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)
. In particular, we have a natural isomorphism
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
induced by restriction. Thus we shall work with
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
instead.

Admissible representatives

In our application to BCILT, we will take representatives of cohomology classes that satisfy the conditions summarized in the following definition:

Definition 2.3
.

A
1
-form
\omega
on
\Sigma
is
admissible
if:

(1)

\omega
is smooth on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
with
d\omega=0
pointwise.

(2)

Near each
z_{i}
,
\omega
is
L^{1}
and
d\omega=m_{i}\delta_{z_{i}}
,
d{*}\omega=0
in the sense of distributions, where
m_{i}=\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega
,
\delta_{z}
denotes the Dirac measure at
z
.

(3)

\omega
is Neumann extendible. In particular,
i_{\nu}\omega|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
.

(4)

In a neighborhood of each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})^{*}\omega=\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}k_{i}\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta
, where
k_{i}=\int_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}}\omega
.

(5)

In a neighborhood of each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{*}\omega=0
.

(6)

\int_{c}\omega\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for any circle
c
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
.

A function
f
on
\Sigma
is
admissible
if:

(1)

f
is smooth on
\Sigma
and harmonic near
{\mathbf{z}}
.

(2)

f
is Neumann extendible. In particular,
\partial_{\nu}f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
.

(3)

In a neighborhood of
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
,
f
is locally constant and takes values in
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

If
f
is an admissible function, then
df
is an admissible
1
-form. The converse is false.

The following lemma describes the behavior of admissible
1
-forms near
{\mathbf{z}}
:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\omega
be an
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}

1
-form on a simply connected neighborhood
D
of
0
in
{\mathbb{C}}
such that
d\omega=m\delta_{0}
,
d{*}\omega=0
in the sense of distributions, where
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then there exists a harmonic function
h
on
D
such that
\omega=m\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta+dh
, where
\theta
is the argument on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus 0
.

Proof.

First we check that
dd\theta=2\pi\delta_{0}
,
d{*}d\theta=0
in the sense of distributions. Clearly
dd\theta=d{*}d\theta=0
on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus 0
. For a test function
f\in{\mathcal{D}}(D)
with
\operatorname{supp}f\subset D_{0}=\{z:|z|\leq\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon>0
small, by integration by parts,

\displaystyle\int_{D}d\theta\wedge df=-\int_{D_{0}}\partial_{r}f\,dr\wedge d\theta=-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\partial_{r}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(re^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta\right){\mathrm{d}}r=2\pi f(0),

\displaystyle\int_{D}d\theta\wedge*df=-\int_{D_{0}}\partial_{\theta}f\,\frac{1}{r}dr\wedge d\theta=-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{r}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}f(re^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta\,{\mathrm{d}}r=0.

Let
\omega
be as in the lemma. Then
\omega_{0}=\omega-m\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta
satisfies
d\omega_{0}=d{*}\omega_{0}=0
. By elliptic regularity,
\omega_{0}
is smooth. Since
D
is simply connected,
\omega_{0}=dh
for some function
h
on
D
. Then
\Delta h=d^{*}\omega=0
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

(1)

Any cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
has an admissible representative
\omega
.

(2)

Any cohomology class
[df]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
has a representative with an admissible primitive
f
.

Proof.

By doubling, we may assume
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, so that
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. Fix a conformal metric
g
on
\Sigma
. For each
z_{i}
, let
\xi_{i}
be a holomorphic parametrization of a neighborhood of
z_{i}
.

(1): Let
\omega
be any smooth representative of a cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. Clearly we may take
\omega
Neumann extendible. In a neighborhood of each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
\omega-(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})_{*}(\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}k_{i}\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta)
(where
k_{i}=\int_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}}\omega
) is exact, so
\omega=(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})_{*}(\varsigma_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}k_{i}\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta)+df_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
for some smooth function
f_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
defined near
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
. Similarly,
\omega=df_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
for some smooth function
f_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
defined near each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
, and
\omega=(\zeta_{i}^{\rm P})_{*}(m_{i}\frac{1}{2\pi}d\theta)+df_{i}^{\rm P}
(where
m_{i}=\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega
,
\zeta_{i}^{\rm P}
is a holomorphic parametrization of a neighborhood of
z_{i}
) for some smooth function
f_{i}^{\rm P}
defined near each
z_{i}
. Let
f
be a Neumann extendible smooth function on
\Sigma
such that
f=f_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
near each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
f=f_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
near each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
f=f_{i}^{\rm P}
near each
z_{i}
. Then
\omega-df
is an admissible representative for
[\omega]
.

(2): Let
f
be any smooth primitive for a cohomology class
[df]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. With
\chi_{i}^{\prime}
,
\chi
as above, let
f_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\chi_{i}^{\prime}f|_{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}}
, then
\chi f_{0}+(1-\chi)f
is an admissible primitive for
[df]
.
∎

Let
g
be a conformal metric on
\Sigma
. We will need to consider the
L^{2}
-norm of an admissible
1
-form
\omega
on
\Sigma
. Since
d\theta
is not
L^{2}
near
0
, we introduce the regularization

\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\int_{\Sigma\setminus\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}D_{g}(z_{i},\varepsilon)}\!|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}^{2}\log\varepsilon\right),

where
m_{i}=\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega
(i.e.,
[\omega]\in H_{\mathbf{m}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
),
D_{g}(z,r)
denotes the geodesic disk of radius
r
centered at
z
with respect to
g
. It is straightforward to check that this converges. For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, we have (see
[
32
, Lemma 3.11]
)

\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{e^{\rho}g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}=\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}^{2}\rho(z_{i}).

Harmonic representatives

It will often be convenient to take harmonic representatives of cohomology classes.

Definition 2.6
.

A
1
-form
\omega
on
\Sigma
is
almost harmonic
if it is Neumann extendible,
L^{1}
, and
d\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}\delta_{z_{i}}
,
d{*}\omega=0
on all of
\Sigma
in the sense of distributions.

Lemma 2.7
.

(1)

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, any cohomology class in
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
has a unique almost harmonic representative.

(2)

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, for any admissible
1
-form
\omega
on
\Sigma
, there exists a unique smooth function
f
on
\Sigma
with
f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
such that
\omega+df
is almost harmonic.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be an admissible representative of a cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
.

Existence: Fix a Neumann extendible conformal metric
g
on
\Sigma
. For a smooth function
f
on
\Sigma
,
\omega+df
is almost harmonic if and only if
d{*}df=d{*}\omega
, i.e.,
\Delta_{g}f=d^{*}\omega
. Now
d^{*}\omega
is smooth on
\Sigma
with
d^{*}\omega=0
near
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\cup{\mathbf{z}}
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, then
\int_{\Sigma}d^{*}\omega\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=-\int_{\Sigma}d{*}\omega=0
, and we take
f=\Delta_{g}^{-1}d^{*}\omega
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we take
f=h-P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}h|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
where
h=\Delta_{g}^{-1}d^{*}\omega
.

Uniqueness: The difference of two almost harmonic forms in the same cohomology class in
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
has the form
dh
with
d{*}dh=0
, i.e.,
h
is harmonic. In particular, it is smooth on
\Sigma
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, then
h
is constant, so
dh=0
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
