, then by assumption,
dh=d(f_{1}-f_{2})
where
f_{1}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=f_{2}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
, so
h|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
is constant, thus
h
is constant, so again
dh=0
.
∎

Remark
.

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, harmonic
1
-forms or functions on
\Sigma
are generally not admissible, since admissibility requires them to be constant in a neighborhood of
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. This latter condition is important for gluing (see the next subsection).

For the summability of BCILT correlation functions, we need:

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
. For
{\mathbf{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
a>0
, we have
\sum_{[\omega]\in H_{\mathbf{m}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})}e^{-a\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega^{\mathrm{h}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}<\infty
, where
\omega^{\mathrm{h}}
denotes the unique almost harmonic representative in
[\omega]
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}(\Sigma)=\{\omega^{\mathrm{h}}:[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma)=H_{\mathbf{0}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\}
, which is a finite-dimensional lattice. Fix
[\omega_{0}]\in H_{\mathbf{m}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. The sum is
\sum_{\omega^{\mathrm{h}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}(\Sigma)}e^{-a\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}+\omega^{\mathrm{h}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}
. Now
\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}+\omega^{\mathrm{h}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\Sigma}|\omega^{\mathrm{h}}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+2\int_{\Sigma}\langle\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{h}},\omega^{\mathrm{h}}\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
, where
\int_{\Sigma}\langle\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{h}},\cdot\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
is a linear functional on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}(\Sigma)
, so this clearly converges.
∎

2.7
Gluing

Gluing and cutting are inverse operations that produce new extended surfaces. In this subsection, we study the change of topology under these operations, in preparation for
Sections

4
and
5
. We are interested in separating families and the groups
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
,
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
.

One can glue two Dirichlet circles or semicircles. One can glue two surfaces or self-glue, i.e., glue two Dirichlet (semi)circles of the same surface. We discuss each of these four cases. For topological considerations, it is necessary to glue one (semi)circle at a time.

Notation
.

For a tuple
{\mathbf{X}}=(X_{i})_{i}
, we write
{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathrm{c}}=(X_{i})_{i\geq 2}
, so that
{\mathbf{X}}=X_{1}\times{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathrm{c}}=X_{1}\times X_{2}\times{\mathbf{X}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}
.
This notation will come in handy in the discussion that follows.

2.7.1
Gluing two surfaces along a circle

Let
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
be two extended surfaces. We add a prime
′
to denote the corresponding object for
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
is outgoing and
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma^{\prime}
is incoming, or vice versa. Let
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
be the surface obtained by gluing
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
and
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}
via
{\zeta_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}\circ(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})^{-1}
(
Fig.

4
). It is naturally an extended surface of type
({\mathfrak{g}}+{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}-2,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{s}}+{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}+{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
. We always denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
the glued (semi)circle, which in this case is the common image of
\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
{\zeta_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}
on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

For notational convenience, suppose
i=j=1
.

•

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
such that
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{i}\neq\varnothing
if and only if
i=1
, and likewise for
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We assume that
{\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}\circ(\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}})^{-1}
sends
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{1}
to
{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}\cap d_{1}^{\prime}
, so that
d_{1}\cup d_{1}^{\prime}
glues to a simple semicircle
d_{1}^{\#}
on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime})\times(d_{1}^{\#}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})
is a separating family of
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

This assumes that
d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}
exist. Otherwise
\Sigma
or
\Sigma^{\prime}
has only one boundary component and no punctures. Then
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime})\times({\mathbf{d}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})
is a separating family of
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

For
{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}-1}
,
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}-1}
, we have a bijection

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H^{1}_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})&\xleftrightarrow{\sim\,}&\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}&H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\times&H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\\
{[\omega]}&\mapsto&\int_{\mathcal{C}}\omega&{[\omega|_{\Sigma}]}&&{[\omega|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}]}\\
{[\omega^{\#}]}&\mapsfrom&&{[\omega]}&&{[\omega^{\prime}]}\end{array}

where in the inverse map, we choose admissible representatives
\omega
,
\omega^{\prime}
of
[\omega]\in H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
,
[\omega^{\prime}]\in H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
, so that the
1
-form
\omega^{\#}
defined to be
\omega
on
\Sigma
and
\omega^{\prime}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is admissible on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

We have a surjective homomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{ccccc}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\times&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})&\to&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})\\
{[df]}&&{[df^{\prime}]}&\mapsto&{[df^{\#}]}\end{array}

where we choose admissible primitives
f
,
f^{\prime}
for
[df]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
,
[df^{\prime}]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
such that
f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}}=0
, so that the function
f^{\#}
defined to be
f
on
\Sigma
and
f^{\prime}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is admissible on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
. Its kernel is
\{([df],[df^{\prime}]):f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}}=0,f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

Remark
.

It is important that admissible
1
-forms and functions are constant
in a neighborhood of
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, so that
\omega^{\#}
,
f^{\#}
here are smooth at
{\mathcal{C}}
. The same remark applies to the other cases below.

Figure 4
:
Gluing two surfaces along a Dirichlet circle

2.7.2
Gluing two surfaces along a semicircle

Let
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
be two extended surfaces, as in the previous case. Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
is outgoing and
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma^{\prime}
is incoming, or vice versa. Let
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
be the surface obtained by gluing
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
and
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}
via
{\zeta_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}\circ(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{-1}
(
Fig.

5
). It is naturally an extended surface of type
({\mathfrak{g}}+{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}^{\prime}+n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\prime}-1-n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}-2,{\mathfrak{s}}+{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{t}}+{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
for
n\in\{0,1\}
, depending on whether the new boundary circle is Neumann or mixed.

Again, suppose
i=j=1
. This case is similar to but simpler than the previous case.

•

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
and likewise for
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime})\times({\mathbf{d}}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
is a separating family of
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

For
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
,
{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}}
, we have a bijection

\begin{array}[t]{ccccc}H^{1}_{{\mathbf{k}}\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})&\xleftrightarrow{\sim\,}&H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\times&H_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\\
{[\omega]}&\mapsto&{[\omega|_{\Sigma}]}&&{[\omega|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}]}\\
{[\omega^{\#}]}&\mapsfrom&{[\omega]}&&{[\omega^{\prime}]}\end{array}

where the inverse map is as in the previous case.

•

We have a surjective homomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{ccccc}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\times&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})&\to&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})\\
{[df]}&&{[df^{\prime}]}&\mapsto&{[df^{\#}]}\end{array}

which is as in the previous case with
f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}}=0
replaced by
f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}}=0
. Its kernel is
\{([df],[df^{\prime}]):f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}}=0,f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=f^{\prime}|_{{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

Figure 5
:
Gluing two surfaces along a Dirichlet semicircle

2.7.3
Self-gluing a surface along a circle

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
is outgoing and
c_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma
is incoming. Let
\#\Sigma
be the surface obtained by gluing
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
and
c_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}
via
\zeta_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}\circ(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})^{-1}
(
Fig.

6
). It is naturally an extended surface of type
({\mathfrak{g}}+1,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}-2,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}},{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
.

For notational convenience, suppose
i=1
,
j=2
.

•

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
such that
d_{1}
goes from
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}
to
c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{i}=\varnothing
for
i\neq 1
,
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{i}=\varnothing
for
i\neq 1,2
. We assume that
\zeta_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}\circ(\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}})^{-1}
sends
c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{1}
to
c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}\cap d_{1}
, so that
d_{1}
glues to a simple circle
d^{\#}_{1}
on
\#\Sigma^{\circ}
. Then
\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\times d^{\#}_{1})\times{\mathbf{d}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}
is a separating family of
\#\Sigma
(recall that
{\mathcal{C}}
denotes the glued circle). (If
d_{2}
does not exist, then
{\mathbf{d}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}
is replaced by the empty tuple.)

•

Fix an admissible function
h
on
\Sigma
with
h|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}=0
,
h|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}=1
. By assumption,
dh=0
near
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, so it glues to a
1
-form
(dh)^{\#}
on
\#\Sigma
. For
{\mathbf{k}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}-2}
, we have a bijection

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H^{1}_{{\mathbf{k}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}}(\#\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\xleftrightarrow{\sim\,}&\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}&H_{k\times k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[\omega]}&\mapsto&\int_{\mathcal{C}}\omega&{[\omega|_{\Sigma}]}&&\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\\
{[\omega^{\#}+n(dh)^{\#}]}&\mapsfrom&&{[\omega]}&&n\end{array}

where in the inverse map, we choose an admissible representative
\omega
of
[\omega]\in H_{k\times k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
with
\int_{d_{1}}\omega=0
, so that it glues to an admissible
1
-form
\omega^{\#}
on
\#\Sigma
.

•

We have a surjective homomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\to&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\#\Sigma)&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[df]}&\mapsto&{[d(f-(f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}})h)]}&&f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}\end{array}

where we choose an admissible primitive
f
for
[df]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, so that
f-(f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}})h
glues to an admissible function (denoted in the same way) on
\#\Sigma
. Its kernel is
\{[df]:f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}\cup c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}=0,f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}=f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

Figure 6
:
Self-gluing a surface along a Dirichlet circle

2.7.4
Self-gluing a surface along a semicircle

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface. Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
is outgoing and
c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset\partial_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\Sigma
is incoming. Let
\#\Sigma
be the surface obtained by gluing
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
and
c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
via
\zeta_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\circ(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{-1}
, which is naturally an extended surface. It is necessary to further distinguish two cases, depending on whether
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
are in the same mixed boundary circle of
\Sigma
.

Case 1.

Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset c_{i^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{M}}
,
c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset c_{j^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{M}}
,
i^{\prime}\neq j^{\prime}
(
Fig.

7
). Then
\#\Sigma
is of type
({\mathfrak{g}}+1,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}-1-n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}-2,{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
for
n\in\{0,1\}
. For notational convenience, suppose
i=i^{\prime}=1
,
j=j^{\prime}=2
.

•

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
such that
d_{1}
goes from
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
to
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}\cap d_{i}=\varnothing
for
i\neq 1
,
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M}}\cap d_{i}=\varnothing
for
i\neq 1,2
,
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cap d_{2}=\varnothing
. We assume that
\zeta_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\circ(\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{-1}
sends
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cap d_{1}
to
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cap d_{1}
, so that
d_{1}
glues to a simple circle
d^{\#}_{1}
on
\#\Sigma^{\circ}
. We move the image of
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
\#\Sigma
slightly to get a smoothly embedded circle
c
on
(\#\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}})\cup d^{\#}_{1}
. Then
\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times c\times d^{\#}_{1})\times(d_{2}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}})
is a separating family of
\#\Sigma
. (If
d_{2}
does not exist, then
d_{2}\times{\mathbf{d}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}
is replaced by the empty tuple.)

•

For
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
, we have a bijection

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H^{1}_{\mathbf{k}}(\#\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\xleftrightarrow{\sim\,}&H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[\omega]}&\mapsto&{[\omega|_{\Sigma}]}&&\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\\
{[\omega^{\#}+n(dh)^{\#}]}&\mapsfrom&{[\omega]}&&n\end{array}

where the inverse map is as in the previous case with
h|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}=0
,
h|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{D}}}=1
replaced by
h|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=0
,
h|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=1
.

•

We have a surjective homomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\to&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\#\Sigma)&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[df]}&\mapsto&{[d(f-(f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}})h)]}&&f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}\end{array}

as in the previous case. Its kernel is
\{[df]:f|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\setminus c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cup c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=0,f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}=f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

Figure 7
:
Self-gluing a surface along a Dirichlet semicircle (case 1)

Case 2.

Suppose
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\cup c_{j}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\subset c_{k}^{\mathrm{M}}
for some
k
(
Fig.

8
). Then
\#\Sigma
is of type
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}},{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}+1-n,{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}-2,{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}})
for
n\in\{0,1,2\}
. Again, suppose
i=1
,
j=2
.

•

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times{\mathbf{d}}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
disjoint from
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
. Then
\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\times({\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathbf{d}})
is a separating family of
\#\Sigma
.

•

Let
d
be a simple semicircle on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
from
c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
to
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
such that
\zeta_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}\circ(\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{-1}
sends
d\cap c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
to
d\cap c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
, so that
d
glues to a simple circle
d^{\#}
on
\#\Sigma
. The homology class of
d^{\#}
in
H_{1}(\#\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
does not depend on the choice of
d
. For
{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
, we have a bijection

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H^{1}_{\mathbf{k}}(\#\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\xleftrightarrow{\sim\,}&H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[\omega]}&\mapsto&{[\omega|_{\Sigma}]}&&\int_{d^{\#}}\omega\\
{[\omega^{\#}+n(dh)^{\#}]}&\mapsfrom&{[\omega]}&&n\end{array}

as in case 1.

•

We have a surjective homomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\to&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\#\Sigma)&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}\\
{[df]}&\mapsto&{[d(f-(f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}})h)]}&&f|_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}-f|_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}\end{array}

as in case 1.

Figure 8
:
Self-gluing a surface along a Dirichlet semicircle (case 2)

3
Construction of the free field

From now on, we fix
R>0
and write
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}={\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
. In this section, we define the measure spaces over which our path integrals take place. More precisely, we give a probabilistic construction of the measure

e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi

on the space of maps
\phi:\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. This is known as the
compactified massless free boson
. It is also a
nonlinear
\sigma
-model
with values in a one-dimensional torus.

Notation
.

We add a bar to denote the projection
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. For example, if
\phi:\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}
, then
\overline{\phi}:\Sigma\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
is its projection to
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. Maintaining this distinction helps to reduce ambiguity.

3.1
Gaussian Free Fields (GFFs)

The probabilistic tool here is the
Gaussian Free Field (GFF)
. We briefly review the main ideas while adopting a more measure-theoretic viewpoint.

The general construction is as follows. Let
(M,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
D
and
\Delta
a nonnegative elliptic operator on
M
of order
k
. We would like to make sense of the measure
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\phi\,\Delta\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi
on the space of functions
\phi:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
. The idea is to think of
\Delta
as an infinite-dimensional positive semidefinite matrix. Let
(e_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}\subset C^{\infty}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
be an
L^{2}
-orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
\Delta
with
\Delta e_{j}=\lambda_{j}e_{j}
, where
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
is nondecreasing in
j
. Here if
\partial M\neq\varnothing
, one imposes a boundary condition, which essentially amounts to choosing a self-adjoint extension of
\Delta
on
L^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
. Suppose
\dim\ker\Delta=d
, so that
\lambda_{1}=\cdots=\lambda_{d}=0<\lambda_{d+1}\leq\lambda_{d+2}\leq\cdots\nearrow\infty
. The
GFF
of
\Delta
is defined by the series

X_{\Delta}=\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\,e_{j},

where the
a_{j}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians. By Weyl’s law,
\lambda_{j}\sim Cj^{k/D}
for some constant
C
as
j\to\infty
. By elliptic regularity, the
L^{2}
Sobolev space of order
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
on
M
is
H^{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}})=\bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}c_{j}e_{j}:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{2s/k}c_{j}^{2}<\infty\bigr\}\subset{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
. Thus
X_{\Delta}
converges a.s. in
H^{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
for
s<(k-D)/2
. In particular, it defines a random distribution on
M
. Let
{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}}):\forall\,f\in\ker\Delta,\int_{\Sigma}uf\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=0\}
, so that
X_{\Delta}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
a.s. We have a decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})=\ker\Delta\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
. The covariance of
X_{\Delta}
is the Green’s function
G_{\Delta}
of
\Delta
orthogonal to
\ker\Delta
, i.e., the Schwartz kernel of the inverse of
\Delta:{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
:

{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\Delta}(x)X_{\Delta}(x^{\prime})]=G_{\Delta}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}e_{j}(x)e_{j}(x^{\prime}).

Identifying
\ker\Delta
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
using the basis
(e_{j})_{j=1}^{d}
, we equip
{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
with the probability measure
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}
induced by
X_{\Delta}
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}={\mathrm{d}}c^{d}\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}
where
{\mathrm{d}}c^{d}
is the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Intuitively, the total mass of
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\phi\,\Delta\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi
on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
is
\prod_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_{j}}}
, which can be formalized as
(\det^{\prime}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta)^{-1/2}
, where
\det^{\prime}
denotes the
regularized determinant
of Ray–Singer. Thus we define
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\phi\,\Delta\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi=(\det^{\prime}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta)^{-1/2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}
.

We are interested in the following cases:

•

M={\mathbb{T}}
,
g=d\theta^{2}
,
\Delta=\frac{1}{\pi}{\mathbf{D}}
. We denote the GFF by
\varphi
. Explicitly,

\varphi(\theta)=\sum_{n>0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big(x_{n}\cos(n\theta)-y_{n}\sin(n\theta)\big)=\sum_{n\neq 0}\varphi_{n}e^{{\mathrm{i}}n\theta},

where the
x_{n},y_{n}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians,
\varphi_{n}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}(x_{n}+{\mathrm{i}}y_{n})
,
\varphi_{-n}=\overline{\varphi_{n}}
for
n>0
. Its covariance is
5
5

5

By abuse of notation, we write functions on
{\mathbb{T}}
or
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
as functions of
\theta
.

{\mathbb{E}}[\varphi(\theta)\varphi(\theta^{\prime})]=-\log|e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}}|.

We equip
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
with the probability measure
{\mathrm{d}}\varphi
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}={\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi
where
\widetilde{\varphi}=c+\varphi
.

•

M={\mathbb{T}}_{+}
,
g=d\theta^{2}
,
\Delta=\frac{1}{\pi}{\mathbf{D}}
with Neumann boundary condition. We denote the GFF by
\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}
. Explicitly,

\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}(\theta)=\sum_{n>0}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\,x_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\cos(n\theta)=\sum_{n\neq 0}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}n\theta},

where the
x_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians,
\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}x_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}
,
\varphi_{-n}^{\mathrm{h}}=\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}
for
n>0
. Note that the coefficients differ from the previous case by
\sqrt{2}
, since
\operatorname{vol}{\mathbb{T}}=2\operatorname{vol}{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
. Its covariance is

{\mathbb{E}}[\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}(\theta)\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}(\theta^{\prime})]=-2\log|e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}}|.

We equip
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
with the probability measure
{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{h}}={\mathrm{d}}c^{\mathrm{h}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}
where
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{h}}=c^{\mathrm{h}}+\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}
.

•

M=\Sigma
is an extended surface,
g
is a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
,
\Delta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{g}
with mixed boundary condition. We denote the GFF by
X_{g}
. Its covariance is

{\mathbb{E}}[X_{g}(x)X_{g}(x^{\prime})]=2\pi\,G_{g}(x,x^{\prime}).

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we have

X_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}\frac{X_{g^{\#2}}+X_{g^{\#2}}\circ\tau}{\sqrt{2}}.

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, we equip
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with the probability measure
{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}
. For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible, we have

X_{e^{\rho}g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}X_{g}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}.

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
, we equip
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with the probability measure
{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}
. There is no zero mode, and the law of
X_{g}
is conformally invariant.

In all of these cases, the GFF converges in
H^{s}
for
s<0
and is a.s. not in
H^{0}=L^{2}
. Note that here the zero mode (corresponding to
\ker\Delta
) is not
L^{2}
-normalized. This normalization will manifest itself as multiplicative constants in the formulas later.

Returning to the general theory, we state some standard results on GFFs that will be used in calculations.

Theorem 3.1

(
Cameron–Martin
)
.

For
h\in H_{\Delta}^{k/2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})=H^{k/2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
, the translated measure
{\mathrm{d}}(X_{\Delta}-h)
is equivalent to
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative given by

{\mathrm{d}}(X_{\Delta}-h)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}h\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right){\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}.

Here the term
\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
is not to be interpreted pointwise but as follows. Using the notation above, for
h=\sum c_{j}e_{j}
,
\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\sum\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\,c_{j}a_{j}\,e_{j}
. By assumption,
\sum\lambda_{j}c_{j}^{2}<\infty
, so this converges a.s. and in
L^{2}
. For a more general statement, we refer to
[
11
, Proposition 2.4.2]
.

Corollary 3.2
.

For
h\in H^{k/2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
, the translated measure
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}(\cdot-h)
is equivalent to
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative given by

{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}(X-h)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}h\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{M}X\,\Delta h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}(X).

In probabilistic language, it can be stated in the following way:

Theorem 3.3

(
Girsanov transform
)
.

For
F\in L^{1}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}}))
,
Y
a centered real Gaussian variable, we have

{\mathbb{E}}[F(X_{\Delta})\,e^{Y}]=e^{\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{2}]}\,{\mathbb{E}}[F(X_{\Delta}+{\mathbb{E}}[YX_{\Delta}])].

Proof.

Take
h={\mathbb{E}}[YX_{\Delta}]
in the Cameron–Martin theorem.
∎

We also need an imaginary version where
Y
is replaced by
{\mathrm{i}}Y
. For this to make sense, we introduce the following function class. Let
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}}))
be the space of functions
F:{\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
of the form
F(X)=P(\langle X,h_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle X,h_{m}\rangle)
where
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
P
is a complex polynomial,
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}\in{\mathcal{D}}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
. For
h\in H_{\Delta}^{k/2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
,
F(\cdot+{\mathrm{i}}h)
makes sense for
F\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}}))
.

Theorem 3.4

(
Imaginary Girsanov transform
)
.

For
F\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\Delta}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}}))
,
Y
a centered real Gaussian variable, we have

{\mathbb{E}}[F(X_{\Delta})\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}Y}]=e^{-\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{2}]}\,{\mathbb{E}}[F(X_{\Delta}+{\mathrm{i}}\,{\mathbb{E}}[YX_{\Delta}])].

The next theorem allows us to compare the GFFs associated to different operators, which is essentially a special case of the Feldman–Hájek theorem:

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\Delta
,
\widetilde{\Delta}
be two strictly positive elliptic operators on
M
such that
\widetilde{\Delta}\Delta^{-1}-I
is smoothing (i.e., it extends to a continuous map
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
). Then
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
is equivalent to
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative given by

{\mathrm{d}}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}=\sqrt{\det\nolimits_{\mathrm{Fr}}(\widetilde{\Delta}\Delta^{-1})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,(\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta)X_{\Delta}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta},

where
\det_{\mathrm{Fr}}
denotes the Fredholm determinant.

Proof.

Let
\Pi_{n}
be the projection from
L^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
to the finite subspace
\mathcal{H}_{n}
spanned by
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
, the first
n
orthonormal basis of
\Delta
. Define
\Delta_{n},\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}
as
\Pi_{n}\circ\Delta\circ\Pi_{n}
and
\Pi_{n}\circ\widetilde{\Delta}\circ\Pi_{n}
. Then, by the knowledge of the Gaussian distribution,

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}_{n}}}=\sqrt{\det\nolimits(\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}\Delta^{-1}_{n})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X_{\Delta_{n}}\,(\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}-\Delta_{n})X_{\Delta_{n}}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}.

For a suitable test function
h
constructed from
e_{0},\ldots,e_{N}
where
N<n
,
\int_{M}X_{\Delta}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{M}X_{\Delta_{n}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
according to the construction of GFF. Therefore, we can study the distribution of
\int_{M}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
using
{\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}}
, and

\begin{split}&\int e^{{\mathrm{i}}\int_{M}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}{\mathrm{d}}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}\\
=&\int e^{{\mathrm{i}}\int_{M}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\sqrt{\det\nolimits(\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}\Delta^{-1}_{n})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,(\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}-\Delta_{n})X_{\Delta}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}{\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}}\\
=&\int e^{{\mathrm{i}}\int_{M}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}}}{{\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}_{n}}}{\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}_{n}}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}h\ (\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})^{-1}\Pi_{n}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}},\end{split}

where on the last line,
\int_{M}h\ (\widetilde{\Delta}_{n}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})^{-1}\Pi_{n}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
converges to
\int_{M}h(x)G_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(x,x^{\prime})h(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
. Next, we claim that the first line converges to

\int e^{{\mathrm{i}}\int_{M}X_{\widetilde{\Delta}}h\ {\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\sqrt{\det\nolimits_{\mathrm{Fr}}(\widetilde{\Delta}\Delta^{-1})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X_{\Delta}\,(\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta)X_{\Delta}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\ {\mathrm{d}}X_{{\Delta}}.

Since
\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta
is smoothing, this follows from the discussion in
[
30
, Lemma 5.3]
.
∎

We will need the following generalization to operators with nonzero kernels. For an operator
\Delta
, we write
\Delta_{0}=\Delta+\Pi_{\Delta}
where
\Pi_{\Delta}
is the orthogonal projection on
L^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
onto
\ker\Delta
. Note that
\Delta_{0}=\Delta
if and only if
\ker\Delta=0
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
\Delta
,
\widetilde{\Delta}
be two nonnegative elliptic operators on
M
such that
\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta
is smoothing. Then
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
is equivalent to
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative given by

{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(X)=(2\pi)^{\frac{\dim\ker\widetilde{\Delta}-\dim\ker\Delta}{2}}\sqrt{\det\nolimits_{\mathrm{Fr}}(\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}\Delta_{0}^{-1})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X\,(\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta)X\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}(X).

Proof.

By definition,

{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta_{0}}=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{j}^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}c^{d}\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\Delta}=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}X\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{\Delta}}X\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\Delta}(X),

where
d=\dim\ker\Delta
,
X=\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{j}e_{j}+X_{\Delta}
. Likewise for
\widetilde{\Delta}
. Clearly
\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}\Delta_{0}^{-1}-I=(\widetilde{\Delta}-\Delta+\Pi_{\widetilde{\Delta}}-\Pi_{\Delta})\Delta_{0}^{-1}
is smoothing, hence Hilbert–Schmidt. Thus the formula follows from the Feldman–Hájek theorem for
\Delta_{0}
,
\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}
.
∎

3.2
The spaces
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

We would like to define the spaces of
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
-valued distributions on
{\mathbb{T}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
. This is a toy example for the next subsection, and they also play a role in the gluing of amplitudes. Taking inspiration from the decomposition (recall that the bar denotes the projection
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
)

\begin{array}[t]{ccccccc}{\mathbb{T}}_{R}&\times&C_{0}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})&\times&{\mathbb{Z}}&\xrightarrow{\sim}&C({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
c&&\varphi&&k&\mapsto&c+\overline{\varphi}+\overline{kR\theta}\end{array}

where
C_{0}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})=\{\varphi\in C({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta=0\}=C({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})
, we define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathbb{Z}}.

We equip it with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}={\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}k
where
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}=(c,\varphi,k)
,
{\mathrm{d}}c
is the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
,
{\mathrm{d}}k
is the counting measure on
{\mathbb{Z}}
. By the decomposition above, we view
C({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
naturally as a subspace of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
.

Similarly, we define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}}),

and equip it with the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{h}}={\mathrm{d}}c^{\mathrm{h}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\mathrm{h}}
where
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\mathrm{h}}=(c^{\mathrm{h}},\varphi^{\mathrm{h}})
(
{\mathrm{h}}
for “half”). By doubling, we view
C({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
naturally as a subspace of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Note that there is no discrete part
{\mathbb{Z}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
because
\overline{kR\theta}
is even if and only if
k=0
. This reflects the fact that
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
is topologically trivial.

Finally, for an extended surface
\Sigma
, we define
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}

and equip it with the product measure
. For

{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}^{k_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{k_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}},\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}^{\mathrm{h}},\ldots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{h}})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),

we have a decomposition
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}={\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}+{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}=(c_{1}^{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}^{k_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}},c_{1}^{\mathrm{h}},\ldots,c_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{h}})\in({\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathbb{Z}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}},\varphi_{1}^{\mathrm{h}},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}^{\mathrm{h}})\in{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}.

The Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}}_{+},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
are the
state spaces
of BCILT.

3.3
The space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
(
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
)

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
g
a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
. We would like to define the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
of
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
-valued distributions
\phi
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
satisfying the Neumann boundary condition
\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{\partial\Sigma}=0
(with
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{N}}\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
). Let (recall
2.3
)

C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}):\tfrac{1}{2\pi R}d\phi\text{ admissible}\}.

Here we trivialize the tangent bundle of
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
using the Lie group structure, so that
d\phi
is a closed
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. Note that the admissibility of
d\phi
implies
\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{\partial\Sigma}=i_{\nu}d\phi|_{\partial\Sigma}=0
. We equip this space with the
C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}
topology on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. We define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})/\!\sim

where
(Y_{1},\psi_{1})\sim(Y_{2},\psi_{2})
if
Y_{1}-Y_{2}\in C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\overline{Y_{1}-Y_{2}}=\psi_{2}-\psi_{1}
. This is the quotient by the closed subspace
\{(Y,-\overline{Y}):Y\in C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}}),\int_{\Sigma}Y\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=0\}
, so it is Hausdorff.

For
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
and
\omega
a closed
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
, we denote by
I_{x_{0}}(\omega)
the multi-valued function on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
defined by
x\mapsto\int_{\gamma}\omega
where
\gamma
is any path on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
from
x_{0}
to
x
. If
\int_{c}\omega\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for any circle
c
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
, then
I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)
projects to a well-defined map
\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)}:\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
with
\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)}=\omega
. This defines a one-to-one correspondence between the homotopy classes of maps
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
and the cohomology classes in
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, i.e., a bijection
\pi_{0}(C(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))\xleftrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, which motivates the following decomposition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
:

Proposition 3.7
.

Fix
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
and choose an admissible representative
\omega
in each cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. We have a homeomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{ccccccc}{\mathbb{T}}_{R}&\times&{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\times&H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
c&&X_{g}&&[\omega]&\mapsto&[(X_{g},c+\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)})]\end{array}

On the left-hand side, consider the measure

\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}}}\exp\left(-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-R\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,d^{*}\omega\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right){\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega],

where
{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}
is the measure induced by the GFF
X_{g}
(recall
Section

3.1
)
,
{\mathrm{d}}c
is the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
,
{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]
is the counting measure on
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. Pushing forward this measure defines a measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
that does not depend on the choices of
x_{0}
or the representatives
\omega
. We denote it by
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}
where
\phi_{g}=[(X_{g},c+\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)})]
.

Here the notation
X_{g}
alludes to the GFF and emphasizes the dependence on the metric
g
. From the measure-theoretic viewpoint, it denotes a deterministic element in the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{0}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, the measure on which depends on
g
. From the probabilistic viewpoint, it can be thought of as a random distribution sampled from the GFF with respect to
g
.

Proof.

The inverse map is
[(Y,\psi)]\mapsto\textstyle(\psi(x_{0})+\overline{\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}},Y+f-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},[\omega])
, where
\omega
is the chosen representative of
[\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\psi]\in H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
,
f=I_{x_{0}}\big(\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\psi-\omega\big)\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. Continuity is trivial.

Changing
x_{0}
amounts to a translation in
c
, i.e.,
[(X_{g},c+I_{x_{0}^{\prime}}(2\pi R\omega)]=[(X_{g},(c-\overline{\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}2\pi R\omega})+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))]
. Suppose we change a representative
\omega
to
\omega+\frac{1}{2\pi R}df
. By admissibility,
f\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. We have
[(X_{g},c+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega+df))]=[(X_{g}+(f-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}),(c+\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-f(x_{0}))+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))]
, so this amounts to a translation in
c
and the translation by
h=f-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
on the GFF
X_{g}
. By the Cameron–Martin theorem,

{\mathrm{d}}(X_{g}+h)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|dh|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,\Delta_{g}h\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right){\mathrm{d}}X_{g}.

Now

\displaystyle\left(-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega+\tfrac{1}{2\pi R}df|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-R\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,d^{*}(\omega+\tfrac{1}{2\pi R}df)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right)-\left(-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-R\int_{\Sigma}(X_{g}+h)\,d^{*}\omega\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|df|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,\Delta_{g}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

so the factors cancel out, as expected.
∎

Remark
.

While it is necessary to put the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
for translation invariance, one can put any measure on
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
. In particular, putting a Dirac measure on
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
amounts to restricting the path integral to fields in a given homotopy class. The resulting theories are well-defined but generally not diffeomorphism invariant in the sense of Quantum Field Theory.

For
{\mathbf{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, let
C_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
be the subspace of maps
\phi\in C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
with winding number
m_{i}
around
z_{i}
, i.e.,
\phi_{*}[c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}]=m_{i}
, where
\phi_{*}:H_{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\to H_{1}({\mathbb{T}}_{R})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
is the homomorphism on the first homology induced by
\phi
, and
c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}
is the boundary of a small disk centered at
z_{i}
(as in
Section

2.6
). We define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times C_{\mathbf{m}}^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})/\!\sim\,\subset{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}).

The homeomorphism above is compatible with the subscript
m
in the sense that it restricts to a homeomorphism
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times H_{\mathbf{m}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, and the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
restricts to a measure on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, denoted in the same way.

Proposition 3.8

(
Conformal covariance
)
.

For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible, we have on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{e^{\rho}g}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{96\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{R^{2}}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}^{2}\rho(z_{i})\right){\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}.

Proof.

Fix
x_{0},\omega
as in
3.7
. For a Neumann extendible metric
g
on
\Sigma
, let
\Phi_{g}:{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{0,g}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
be the associated homeomorphism with respect to
g
, where the subscript
g
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{0,g}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
denotes dependence on
g
. Then
\Phi_{e^{\rho}g}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{g}:(c,X_{g},[\omega])\mapsto(c+\overline{\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}},X_{g}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g},[\omega])
. Recall that
X_{e^{\rho}g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}X_{g}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}
. Thus
{\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{e^{\rho}g}\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]={\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]
. The conformal covariance of
\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
gives the second factor. By the Polyakov formula (
[
34
, Proposition 5.10]
),

\frac{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{e^{\rho}g}}{\operatorname{vol}_{e^{\rho}g}\Sigma}=\frac{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\,\exp\left(-\frac{1}{48\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\big(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right),

which gives the first factor.
∎

Remark
.

Without the assumption that
g
is Neumann extendible (so that
\partial\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma
is geodesic), one should multiply the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
by
e^{\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}}
to have the correct Polyakov formula.

3.4
The space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
(
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
)

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
and
g
a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
. For
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

(recall
Section

3.2
)
, we would like to define the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
of
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
-valued distributions
\phi
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
satisfying the mixed boundary condition
\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
,
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
. The definition is more intricate due to two things:

•

It is not obvious how to define the boundary value of a distribution. Moreover, the boundary value
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
here is highly singular (not even in
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}
).

•

The solution to the previous problem in the real theory was to define the field as the Dirichlet GFF plus the harmonic extension of the boundary value. However, in our case, the field takes values in
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
, and harmonic extensions (in the sense of harmonic maps) are not unique. This nonuniqueness manifests itself as the cohomology
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
(recall
Section

2.6
) below.

Our strategy is to separate the singular part and the topological part in the boundary value
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
. First let

C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}):\tfrac{1}{2\pi R}d\phi\text{ admissible}\}.

This is formally the same as
C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
for the case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
. Here the admissibility of
d\phi
implies:

•

\partial_{\nu}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=i_{\nu}d\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}=0
, as before.

•

In a neighborhood of each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
,
(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{D}})^{*}\phi-\overline{k_{i}R\theta}
is constant, where
k_{i}=\phi_{*}[c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}]
is the winding number of
\phi
on
c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}
.

•

In a neighborhood of each
c_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
,
(\zeta_{i}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}})^{*}\phi
is constant.

We equip this space with the
C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}
topology on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. Let

C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus({\mathbf{z}}\cup{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma),{\mathbb{T}}_{R}):{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}\text{ exists},\phi-\overline{P_{\Sigma}\Pi{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}}\in C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\},

where the trace
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
is in the weak sense (see
Section

2.4
),
\Pi
is the projection
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}={\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}+{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Note that the maps in
C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
are generally singular at
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. By definition, we have a bijection
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),({\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\psi)\mapsto\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\psi
. We equip
C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
with the pushforward of the product topology, so that the trace map
C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),\phi\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
is a continuous surjection. For
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, let

\displaystyle C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

\displaystyle=\{\phi\in C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}):{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\psi:\psi\in C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\psi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}\}

\displaystyle=\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),

which is a closed subspace of
C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Note that
C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\bigsqcup_{{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}}C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, hence the notation. Finally, we define

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times C^{\mathrm{M}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})/\!\sim

where
(Y_{1},\psi_{1})\sim(Y_{2},\psi_{2})
if
Y_{1}-Y_{2}\in C(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with
(Y_{1}-Y_{2})|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
and
\overline{Y_{1}-Y_{2}}=\psi_{2}-\psi_{1}
. This is the quotient by the closed subspace
\{(Y,-\overline{Y}):Y\in C^{{\mathrm{M}},\mathbf{0}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\}
, so it is Hausdorff. For
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})/\!\sim

\displaystyle=\{[(Y,\psi)]\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}):{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}\psi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\},

which is a closed subspace of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
.

Unlike the previous case,
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
is insufficient to determine the homotopy type of a map in
C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, i.e., two maps in
C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
that are homotopic in
C(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
are not necessarily homotopic in
C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Indeed, the kernel of the natural homomorphism
\pi_{0}(C^{{\mathrm{M}},\mathbf{0}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))\to\pi_{0}(C(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
is isomorphic to
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, so there is a (noncanonical) bijection
\pi_{0}(C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))\cong H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
, which motivates the following decomposition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
:

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. We make the following choices:

•

Fix
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
.

•

Take
c_{0}={\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{-1}(x_{0}))\in{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
if
x_{0}\in{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, otherwise take any
c_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
.

•

Choose an admissible representative
\omega
in each cohomology class
[\omega]\in H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
such that
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}+c_{0}={\mathbf{c}}^{\mathbf{k}}
.

•

Choose an admissible primitive
f
for each cohomology class
[df]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
.

We have a homeomorphism

\begin{array}[t]{ccccccc}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\times&H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)&\times&H^{1}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})&\to&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
X_{g}&&[df]&&[\omega]&\mapsto&[(X_{g},\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\overline{2\pi Rf}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)+c_{0})]\end{array}

On the left-hand side, consider the measure

\frac{e^{\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}}}{\sqrt{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}}}\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle{\boldsymbol{\varphi}},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}}){\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle}\exp\left(-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega+df|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-R\int_{\Sigma}(X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})\,d^{*}(\omega+df)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right){\mathrm{d}}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}[df]\,{\mathrm{d}}[\omega],

where
{\mathrm{d}}[df]
,
{\mathrm{d}}[\omega]
are the counting measures on
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
,
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, respectively. Pushing forward this measure defines a measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
that does not depend on the choices of
x_{0},c_{0},\omega,f
. We denote it by
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}
where
\phi_{g}=[(X_{g},\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\overline{2\pi Rf}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)+c_{0})]
.

Proof.

The inverse map is
[(Y,\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\psi)]\mapsto([Y+h],[df],[\omega])
, where
\omega
is the chosen representative of
[\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\psi]\in H_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
,
f
is the chosen primitive for
[\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\psi-\omega]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
,
h=I_{x_{0}}\big(\frac{1}{2\pi R}d\psi-\omega\big)-c_{0}-f\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
. Continuity is trivial.

The rest of the proof is similar to
3.7
. We record the formulas for changing the choices:

•

Changing
x_{0}
to
x_{0}^{\prime}
amounts to changing
c_{0}
to
c_{0}+\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\omega
.

•

If
x_{0}\notin{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
, changing
c_{0}
to
c_{0}^{\prime}
forces changing
\omega
to
\omega+dh
with
h\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
admissible,
h(x_{0})=2\pi R(c_{0}-c_{0}^{\prime})
, which amounts to the translation
X_{g}\mapsto X_{g}+2\pi Rh
.

•

Changing
\omega
to
\omega+dh
with
h
admissible,
h(x_{0})=0
amounts to the translation
[df]\mapsto[df]+[dh]
on
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
.

•

Changing
f
to
f+h
with
h\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
admissible amounts to the translation
X_{g}\mapsto X_{g}+2\pi Rh
.∎

Remark
.

Similarly to the remark after
3.7
, while it is necessary to to put the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
and the counting measure on
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
for translation invariance, one can put any measure on
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
.

For
{\mathbf{m}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, we define the corresponding subspaces with the subscript
m
as before, so that the homeomorphism above restricts to a homeomorphism
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\times H_{{\mathbf{k}},{\mathbf{m}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, and the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
restricts to a measure on
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, denoted in the same way.

Proposition 3.10

(
Conformal covariance
)
.

For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible with
\rho|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
, we have on
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbf{m}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})

{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{e^{\rho}g}=\exp\left(\frac{1}{96\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{R^{2}}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}m_{i}^{2}\rho(z_{i})\right){\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}.

Proof.

This is similar to but simpler than
3.8
, since
X_{e^{\rho}g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}X_{g}
in this case.
∎

Remark
.

Our definition is equivalent to that in
[
32
]
in the following sense. Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with Dirichlet boundary only.

•

The topological part in
[
32
]
is
H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},\partial\Sigma)
, which is in bijection with our
H_{{\mathbf{m}},{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)
(recall that
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\cong H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
is the kernel of the natural homomorphism
H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},\partial\Sigma)\to H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
). This bijection is not canonical.

•

In
[
32
]
, the zero mode of the boundary value
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}
is in
{\mathbb{R}}
, and one has to check that the resulting path integral is invariant under translation by
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
. However, since we define the boundary value
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
to have zero mode in
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}={\mathbb{R}}/2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
, this invariance is automatic for us.

4
Gluing for the free field

In this section, we prove Segal’s gluing axiom for the free field, which is really the crux of the matter. Before we state the result, let us explain the idea. We return to the discussion in
Section

1
on general path integrals (with values in a target space
M
). Focusing on the Dirichlet boundary and ignoring the parametrizations
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
, the path integral is

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi:\Sigma\to M\\
\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\end{subarray}}F(\phi)\,e^{-S(\phi)}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi.

Suppose we cut
\Sigma
into two surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
along a simple curve
{\mathcal{C}}
. We consider
{\mathcal{C}}
to be a Dirichlet boundary of both
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
. Write
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{1}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{2}
where
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{i}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}}
. Suppose the Lagrangian
S
is
local
, i.e.,

S(\phi)=S(\phi|_{\Sigma_{1}})+S(\phi|_{\Sigma_{2}}).

Then for a
local
observable
F
on
\Sigma
of the form
F(\phi)=F_{1}(\phi|_{\Sigma_{1}})F_{2}(\phi|_{\Sigma_{2}})
for some observables
F_{i}
on
\Sigma_{i}
, it is natural to expect that

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi:\Sigma\to M\\
\phi|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}={\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\end{subarray}}F(\phi)\,e^{-S(\phi)}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi=\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}:{\mathcal{C}}\to M}\bigg(\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi_{1}:\Sigma_{1}\to M\\
\phi_{1}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma_{1}}=\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{1}\end{subarray}}F_{1}(\phi_{1})\,e^{-S(\phi_{1})}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi_{1}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi_{2}:\Sigma_{2}\to M\\
\phi_{2}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma_{2}}=\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{2}\end{subarray}}F_{2}(\phi_{2})\,e^{-S(\phi_{2})}\,{\mathrm{D}}\phi_{2}\bigg){\mathrm{D}}\widetilde{\varphi},

since both sides integrate over the same space of fields. We shall state this as an isomorphism of measure spaces

\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{C}},M)}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{1}}(\Sigma_{1},M)\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{2}}(\Sigma_{2},M)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}(\Sigma,M)

where we equip the left-hand side with an appropriate product measure.

Instead of cutting, we state the result for the inverse operation of gluing. In each of the four cases discussed in
Section

2.7
, one needs to distinguish between the cases
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
for the glued surface
\Sigma
, since the definitions of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
are fundamentally different. This results in a total of eight cases. To avoid the tedium of stating a separate result for each case, we introduce the following conventions to unify the notation:

•

For an extended surface
\Sigma
with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, we define
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\{*\}
,
C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=C^{\mathrm{N}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},*}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, where
*
denotes the empty tuple. This unifies the cases
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
.

•

We extend all previous definitions naturally to disjoint unions of extended surfaces. For example, if
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
, then
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})={\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma_{1}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}_{1},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma_{2}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}_{2},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. This unifies the cases of gluing two surfaces and self-gluing a surface.

•

We allow arbitrary reordering of the parametrizations in
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
and reindex them as
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(\zeta_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}
. This unifies the cases of gluing along a circle or semicircle.

Let
\Sigma
be either an extended surface or a disjoint union of two extended surfaces with
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
on different connected components. Suppose
\zeta_{1}
is outgoing and
\zeta_{2}
is incoming, and they both parametrize a circle or semicircle. Let
\Sigma^{\#}
be the surface obtained by gluing the images of
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
on
\Sigma
via
\zeta_{2}\circ\zeta_{1}^{-1}
, which is naturally an extended surface with parametrizations
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{0}={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}=(\zeta_{i})_{i\geq 3}
. We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
the common image of
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
on
\Sigma^{\#}
and
\zeta
its induced parametrization. It is either a simple circle with
{\mathcal{C}}\subset(\Sigma^{\#})^{\circ}
or a Neumann extendible simple semicircle with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}\subset(\Sigma^{\#})^{\circ}
,
\partial{\mathcal{C}}\subset{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma^{\#}
. We write
\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbb{T}}
or
{\mathbb{T}}_{+}
accordingly.

We shall need the following interior Poisson and Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators. For
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
, we define
P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}=P_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times\mathbf{0})
,
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}=\zeta_{1}^{*}\partial_{\nu}P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\operatorname{im}\zeta_{1}}+\zeta_{2}^{*}\partial_{\nu}P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}|_{\operatorname{im}\zeta_{2}}
. By integration by parts, for
\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\int_{\Sigma}|dP_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta,

where
g
is any conformal metric on
\Sigma^{\#}
. Note that
P_{\Sigma^{\#}{\mathcal{C}}}1=1
if and only if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing
, so

\ker{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}=\begin{cases}0,&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\\
{\mathbb{R}},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing.\end{cases}

As in
Section

2.4
, we have that
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}-2{\mathbf{D}}
is smoothing.

For a functorial object
u
on
\Sigma^{\#}
, we denote by
u|_{\Sigma}
its pullback to
\Sigma
via the quotient map
\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\#}
. Let
g
be a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma^{\#}
. We equip
\Sigma
with the metric
g|_{\Sigma}
.

We are now ready to state the gluing theorem.

Theorem 4.1

(
Gluing
)
.

For
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{0}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, we have an isomorphism of measure spaces

\begin{array}[t]{cccc}\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}}(\Sigma^{\#}\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
&[(Y,\psi)]&\mapsto&[(Y+P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}}),\psi-\overline{P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})})]\end{array}

where
h_{\mathcal{C}}=\zeta^{*}P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0}|_{\mathcal{C}}
, and the measure on the left-hand side is
C\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
with

C=\begin{cases}(\sqrt{2}\pi)^{-1},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{circle},\\
\sqrt{2},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{semicircle},\\
(2\pi)^{-3/4},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{circle},\\
(2\pi)^{1/4},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{semicircle}.\end{cases}

Comments on the statement:

•

Recall (
Section

3.2
) the decompositions
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}={\mathbf{c}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}+{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0}
,
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}=c^{k}+\varphi
, where
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
if
{\mathcal{C}}
is a circle,
k={\mathrm{h}}
(for “half”) if
{\mathcal{C}}
is a semicircle. By definition,
P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0}|_{\Sigma}=P_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0})
. Here
\psi\in\overline{P_{\Sigma}(\varphi\times\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0})}+C^{{\mathrm{M}},c^{k}\times c^{k}\times{\mathbf{c}}_{0}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, so
\psi-\overline{P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})}\in\overline{P_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0})}+C^{{\mathrm{M}},c^{k}\times c^{k}\times{\mathbf{c}}_{0}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
glues to a map in
\overline{P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{0}}+C^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathbf{c}}_{0}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma^{\#}\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
.

•

There is a caveat with the interpretation of
Y+P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
. Since
{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\subsetneq{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
, a general distribution on
\Sigma
does not glue to a distribution on
\Sigma^{\#}
. Here
Y\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
is defined a.s. on
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
: one checks that the series defining the GFF
X_{g|_{\Sigma}}
on
\Sigma
also converges a.s. in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
. As for the other term,
\varphi\in H^{-1/2}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
a.s., so
P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})=L^{2}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
a.s. There is no problem with
P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}
since
h_{\mathcal{C}}
is smooth.

It is straightforward to check that this map is well-defined. Note that it is clearly compatible with the subcript
m
, i.e., it restricts to an isomorphism
\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}}(\Sigma^{\#}\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
.

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of this theorem.

Lemma 4.2
.

We have an isomorphism of measure spaces

\begin{array}[t]{ccccc}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\times&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})\\
X_{g|_{\Sigma}}&&\widetilde{\varphi}&\mapsto&X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}\end{array}

where the measure on the left-hand side is

C\sqrt{\det\nolimits_{\mathrm{Fr}}({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}},0}(2{\mathbf{D}}_{0})^{-1})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle\widetilde{\varphi},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}-2{\mathbf{D}})\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}

with

C=\begin{cases}1/\sqrt{\pi},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{circle},\\
1/\sqrt{2\pi},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{semicircle},\\
\sqrt{2},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing.\end{cases}

Here the subscript
0
has the same meaning as in
3.6
.

Proof.

The inverse map is
Y\mapsto(Y|_{\Sigma}-P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}},\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}})
. By the Markov property of GFFs,
X_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}\zeta^{*}X_{g}|_{\mathcal{C}}
. Thus the measure on the left-hand side should be
{\mathrm{d}}X_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
where
{\mathrm{d}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is the pushforward of the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
to
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
via the map
Y\mapsto\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}
. We claim that
{\mathrm{d}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
coincides with the measure induced by
\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}
where the zero mode is not
L^{2}
-normalized. Then the comparison between
{\mathrm{d}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}
follows from an application of the proof of
3.6
to
\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\frac{1}{\pi}{\mathbf{D}}
, with a different constant due to the normalization of the zero mode. More precisely,
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\frac{1}{\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{0}}=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{vol}\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}}{2\pi^{2}}}\,e^{-\frac{\operatorname{vol}\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}}{2\pi}c^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\frac{1}{\pi}{\mathbf{D}}}
,
{\mathrm{d}}X_{\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}},0}}=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{vol}\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}}{4\pi^{2}}}\,e^{-\frac{\operatorname{vol}\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}}{4\pi}c^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}}
if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing
, hence
C
.

Clearly
\zeta^{*}X_{g}|_{\mathcal{C}}
is a Gaussian field on
\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}}
with covariance
{\mathbb{E}}[\widetilde{\varphi}(\theta)\widetilde{\varphi}(\theta^{\prime})]=2\pi\,G_{g}(\zeta(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}),\zeta(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}}))
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing
, then
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}
is invertible and the Schwartz kernel of
