{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}^{-1}
is
K_{{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}^{-1}}=(\zeta\times\zeta)^{*}G_{g}|_{{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}}
(
[
34
, Lemma 5.6]
), so
\zeta^{*}X_{g}|_{\mathcal{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{law}}}{{=}}X_{\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}}
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing
, then the inverse of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}:{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
is the projection of the operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
with Schwartz kernel
(\zeta\times\zeta)^{*}G_{g}|_{{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{C}}}
to
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
(ibid.), so
{\mathrm{d}}\zeta^{*}(c+X_{g})|_{\mathcal{C}}={\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta^{*}X_{g}|_{\mathcal{C}}={\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}X_{\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

We have

\textstyle\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}=C\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g|_{\Sigma}}\det_{\mathrm{Fr}}({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}},0}(2{\mathbf{D}}_{0})^{-1})

with

C=\begin{cases}2\pi,&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{circle},\\
\sqrt{2\pi},&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{semicircle},\\
\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma,&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{circle},\\
\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma,&{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing,\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\textrm{semicircle}.\end{cases}

Proof.

See
[
34
, Proposition B.3]
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

For
{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{0}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})
,
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle-\langle{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#}}-{\mathbf{D}}){\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt=\langle\widetilde{\varphi},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}-2{\mathbf{D}})\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle-2\langle\widetilde{\varphi},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle+\langle h_{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle,

where
h_{\mathcal{C}}=\zeta^{*}P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}|_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By definition,

\langle\widetilde{\varphi},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}-2{\mathbf{D}})\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times\mathbf{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times\mathbf{0})\rangle.

Since
P_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})=P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}|_{\Sigma}
where
P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}
is smooth at
{\mathcal{C}}
, we have

\zeta_{1}^{*}\partial_{\nu}P_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})|_{\operatorname{im}\zeta_{1}}+\zeta_{2}^{*}\partial_{\nu}P_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})|_{\operatorname{im}\zeta_{2}}=0,

so

\langle\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times\mathbf{0},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times\mathbf{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle=-\langle\widetilde{\varphi},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle.

Similarly,
\langle h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle=0
, so

\langle h_{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle=\langle h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0})\rangle=-\langle h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}(0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle.

We have
\langle 0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle=\langle{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#}}-{\mathbf{D}}){\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}\rangle
, so

\langle 0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})\rangle-\langle{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#}}-{\mathbf{D}}){\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}\rangle=-\langle 0\times 0\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}(h_{\mathcal{C}}\times h_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0})\rangle=\langle h_{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle.

Summing up, we get the desired formula.
∎

With these three lemmas, one can immediately prove gluing for the real theory, which in our notation can be stated as an isomorphism of measure spaces

\begin{array}[t]{cccc}\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{R}})}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}}(\Sigma^{\#},{\mathbb{R}})\\
&P_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{\varphi}\times\widetilde{\varphi}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0})+X_{g|_{\Sigma}}&\mapsto&P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}+(P_{\Sigma^{\#},{\mathcal{C}}}(\widetilde{\varphi}-h_{\mathcal{C}})+X_{g})\end{array}

where
h_{\mathcal{C}}=\zeta^{*}P_{\Sigma^{\#}}{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}|_{\mathcal{C}}
, and the measure on the left-hand side is
C\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}
with the same
C
coming from
4.2
and
4.3
. For BCILT, one still needs to study the gluing of the cohomology part, which has to be done on a case-by-case basis as in
Section

2.7
. We shall explain the idea and give the complete proof only in one case, the other cases being similar.

The gist of the proof is the following. We would like to apply
4.2
, but recall that the zero mode of
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
is in
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. By the discussion in
Section

2.7
, in all cases, we have a surjective map

\bigsqcup_{k\in H^{1}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}})}H^{1}_{\mathrm{D}}(\Sigma)\times H_{k\times k\times{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\to H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\#})\times H_{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\#}\setminus{\mathbf{z}})

If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}\neq\varnothing
, this map has kernel
{\mathbb{Z}}
, which complements the zero mode of
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
, so that the zero mode in the end is really integrated over
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\cong{\mathbb{R}}
(as measure spaces), as in
4.2
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\#}=\varnothing
, this map is bijective, and we restrict the zero mode in
4.2
to
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
, giving exactly the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
for
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
where the zero mode is over
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. This shows that the map in
4.1
is bijective on the set-theoretic level (up to sets of measure zero). The factors in the product measures cancel out by calculations using the Cameron–Martin theorem, similarly to the proofs of
3.7
and
3.9
.

Proof of
4.1
in the case of gluing two surfaces along a Dirichlet circle.

We resume the notations in
Section

2.7.1
. Let
g
be a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})^{\#}=\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
.

The case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})\neq\varnothing
.

Unraveling the notational conventions, the map in the theorem in this case is

\small\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\!(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\times&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}}\!(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}}\!(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
&[(Y,\psi)]&&[(Y^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})]&\mapsto&[(Y+Y^{\prime}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}}),\psi^{\#}-\overline{P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})})]\end{array}

where
h_{\mathcal{C}}=\zeta^{*}P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})|_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\psi^{\#}
is defined to be
\psi
on
\Sigma
and
\psi^{\prime}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

First we explain the topology on the left-hand side. Let
u
be an admissible function on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
u=0
near
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})
,
u=1
near
{\mathcal{C}}
. For
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}=c^{k}+\varphi\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, we have a homeomorphism
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},0^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\!(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\!(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}),[(Y,\psi)]\mapsto[(Y,\psi+P_{\Sigma}(\varphi\times\mathbf{0})+cu|_{\Sigma})]
, and likewise for
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then the left-hand side is in bijection with

{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\times\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\big({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},0^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\!(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},0^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}}\!(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\big),

and we equip it with the pullback of the natural topology on this space. Clearly this topology does not depend on the choice of
u
.

To compare the pullback measure and the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
on the left-hand side, we make the following choices on
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
according to
3.9
(we add
′
for
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
#
for
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
):

•

We choose
x_{0}=\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}(1)\in\Sigma
,
x_{0}^{\prime}={\zeta_{1}^{\mathrm{D}}}^{\prime}(1)\in\Sigma^{\prime}
,
x_{0}^{\#}\in\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
the common image of
x_{0}
,
x_{0}^{\prime}
on the glued circle
{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

We choose
c_{0}=c_{0}^{\prime}=c
(the zero mode of
\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
),
c_{0}^{\#}=0
.

•

In each cohomology class
[\omega^{\#}]\in H_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
with
\int_{\mathcal{C}}\omega^{\#}=k
, we choose an admissible representative
\omega^{\#}
such that
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{0}^{*}I_{x_{0}^{\#}}(2\pi R\omega^{\#})|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})}=({\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}\times({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}
,
\zeta^{*}I_{x_{0}^{\#}}(2\pi R\omega^{\#})|_{{\mathcal{C}}}=0^{k}
, and we take it as the representative for defining the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}}\!(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. For
c\in[0,2\pi R)\cong{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
(as measure spaces),
\omega_{c}=\omega^{\#}|_{\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2\pi R}c\;\!du|_{\Sigma}
is an admissible
1
-form on
\Sigma
with
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega_{c})|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}+c_{0}=c^{k}\times({\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}
, and we take it as the representative for defining the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\!(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Likewise for
\Sigma^{\prime}
. This is well-defined because
H_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\times H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
is a bijection.

•

For each cohomology class
[df^{\#}]\in H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})
, we choose an admissible primitive
f^{\#}
such that
f^{\#}=0
near
{\mathcal{C}}
, and we take it as the representative for defining the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}}\!(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
f_{n}=f^{\#}|_{\Sigma}+n(u|_{\Sigma}-1)
is an admissible function on
\Sigma
, and we take it as the representative for defining the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Likewise for
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Here
{\mathbb{Z}}
comes from the kernel
K={\mathbb{Z}}([du|_{\Sigma}],[du|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}])
of the surjective homomorphism
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})
discussed in
Section

2.7.1
. Choosing primitives for
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
in this way amounts to choosing a section for it. Then primitives for
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
are chosen using the induced bijection
(H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}))/K\times{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
.

With these choices, the map in question becomes

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\varphi}^{k},[(X_{g|_{\Sigma}},\overline{P_{\Sigma}(\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}})}+\overline{2\pi Rf_{n}}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega_{c})+c_{0})],[(X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}},\overline{P_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})}+\overline{2\pi Rf_{n}^{\prime}}+I_{x_{0}^{\prime}}(2\pi R\omega_{c}^{\prime})+c_{0}^{\prime}])

\displaystyle\qquad\mapsto[(X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}}),\overline{P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})}+\overline{2\pi Rf^{\#}_{n}}+I_{x_{0}^{\#}}(2\pi R\omega^{\#}_{c})+c_{0})]

\displaystyle\qquad=[(X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})+(2\pi Rn+c)u,\overline{P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})}+\overline{2\pi Rf^{\#}}+I_{x_{0}^{\#}}(2\pi R\omega^{\#}))]

which decomposes into three independent parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\times[0,2\pi R)\times{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\times H_{k\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}_{{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle(H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)\times H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}))/K\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})

The second and third maps are bijective, so they trivially preserve the counting measures. Since
[0,2\pi R)\times{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}},(c,n)\mapsto 2\pi Rn+c
as measure spaces, the first map is equivalent to
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}}),(X_{g|_{\Sigma}},X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}},\widetilde{\varphi})\mapsto X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}})+cu
, where the zero mode
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This differs from the map in
4.2
by a translation. It remains to collect the factors, i.e., the functions before the product measure on the left-hand side. Write
X_{g}=X_{g|_{\Sigma}}+X_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}\widetilde{\varphi}
,
u^{\prime}=P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}1-u
. By
4.2
and Cameron–Martin, the factor of the pullback measure is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{\textstyle\det_{\mathrm{Fr}}({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(2{\mathbf{D}}_{0})^{-1})}\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle\widetilde{\varphi},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}-2{\mathbf{D}})\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle}\times\frac{e^{\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}}}{\sqrt{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}}}\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}-{\mathbf{D}})({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})\rangle}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega^{\#}+df^{\#}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-R\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}(X_{g}-P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}-cu^{\prime}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}))\,d^{*}(\omega^{\#}+df^{\#})\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right)

\displaystyle\times e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle h_{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle\widetilde{\varphi},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle}\exp\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}|du^{\prime}|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{c}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}\langle d(X_{g}-P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}),du^{\prime}\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right).

By definition, the factor of the product measure
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}
is

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\frac{1}{8\pi}(\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})}}{\sqrt{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g|_{\Sigma}}\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}}}\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma}-{\mathbf{D}})(\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}})\rangle-\frac{1}{4\pi}\langle\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}},({\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}-{\mathbf{D}})(\varphi\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})\rangle)}

\displaystyle\times\exp\bigg({-}\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\big|\omega^{\#}+df^{\#}+\tfrac{c}{2\pi R}du\big|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt-R\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}(X_{g}-P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}-cP_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}))\,d^{*}(\omega_{c}^{\#}+df_{n}^{\#}+\tfrac{c}{2\pi R}du)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\bigg).

The difference between the integrals over
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\frac{c^{2}}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}(|du|_{g}^{2}-|du^{\prime}|_{g}^{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{c}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}\langle d(X_{g}-P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}),du^{\prime}\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\frac{c}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}\langle d(X_{g}-P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}h_{\mathcal{C}}-cP_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}})),du\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{c^{2}}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}|dP_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{c}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}\langle dP_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}(\widetilde{\varphi}-h_{\mathcal{C}}),dP_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}}{4\pi}\langle 1,{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1\rangle+\frac{c}{2\pi}\langle\varphi-h_{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbf{D}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}1\rangle,

where we used integration by parts several times. The other terms cancel out by
4.3
and
4.4
.

The case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})=\varnothing
.

The map in this case is

\begin{array}[t]{cccccc}\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\times&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\xrightarrow{\sim}&{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
&[(Y,\psi)]&&[(Y^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})]&\mapsto&[(Y+Y^{\prime}+P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}\varphi,\psi^{\#}-\overline{P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}}\varphi})]\end{array}

where
\psi^{\#}
is defined to be
\psi
on
\Sigma
and
\psi^{\prime}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We make the same choices as in the previous case. The difference now is that
K=0
. In fact,
H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma)=H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})=H_{\mathrm{D}}^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime})=0
. The map decomposes into two independent parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}})\times[0,2\pi R)\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}H_{k}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\times H_{k}^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})

Since
P_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}}1=1
, we can restrict the zero mode on both sides in
4.2
to
[0,2\pi R)
to get an isomorphism of measure spaces

{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})\times({\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{R}}))\to{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})

which is what we want, since
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\cong{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},{\mathbb{R}})\times H^{1}(\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
. One checks similarly that the factors match.
∎

5
Curvature terms

This section generalizes
[
32
, Section 4]
.

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface and
g
a conformal metric on
\Sigma
. For the definition of BCILT, we need to make sense of the integrals

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

where
\omega
is a closed
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
,
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
,
I_{x_{0}}(\omega)
is as defined in
Section

3.3
. The problem is that
I_{x_{0}}(\omega)
is multi-valued. To circumvent this, the idea is to integrate over a domain of full measure on which
I_{x_{0}}(\omega)
is single-valued. By (2) of
2.2
, such a domain can be obtained by removing a separating family. Then one introduces suitable regularization terms to ensure that the resulting quantity is well-behaved under change of separating family.

The precise definition is as follows. For notational convenience, we relabel the set
C=\{c_{i}^{\mathrm{N}}\}_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}}\sqcup\{c_{i}^{\mathrm{D}}\}_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\sqcup\{c_{i}^{\mathrm{M}}\}_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}}\sqcup\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}
as
\{c_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}}
, where
\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{N}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}+{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M}}+{\mathfrak{s}}
. For
c\in C
, we denote by
[c]
the homology class of
c
in
H_{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
, where
[z_{i}]
is defined to be
-[c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}]
. In much of what follows, the type of an element in
C
is irrelevant.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=(a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{\mathfrak{g}},b_{\mathfrak{g}},d_{1},\ldots,d_{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1})
be a separating family of
\Sigma
(with respect to an implicitly fixed
{\mathbf{v}}
). For each
d_{i}
, let
e_{i}
be a simple circle on
(\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}})\cup d_{i}^{\circ}
such that
e_{i}
intersects
d_{i}
exactly once and the intersection is transversal and positively oriented. The homology class
[e_{i}]
of
e_{i}
does not depend on the choice of
e_{i}
. In fact, we have the following combinatorial description of
[e_{i}]
. Suppose
d_{i}
goes from
c_{s}
to
c_{t}
. Deleting
d_{i}
from the tree formed by the
d_{j}
, it splits into two connected components. Let
C_{s}
be the set of elements in
C
in the connected component of
c_{s}
, and likewise
C_{t}
for
c_{t}
, so that
C=C_{s}\sqcup C_{t}
. Then
[e_{i}]=\sum_{c\in C_{s}}[c]=-\sum_{c\in C_{t}}[c]
. Note that reversing the orientation of
d_{i}
amounts to reversing the orientation of
e_{i}
.

Let
\omega
be a closed
1
-form on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. For
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
, we define
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega):\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\to{\mathbb{R}},x\mapsto\int_{\gamma}\omega
where
\gamma
is any path on
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
from
x_{0}
to
x
. By
2.2
, it is well-defined, i.e., single-valued. It is smooth on
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
and has jumps at
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
given by the cycles of
\omega
. We assume that
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
is bounded, so that
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)|_{\partial\Sigma}\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
, since
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cap\partial\Sigma
is finite. Then we define

\displaystyle K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\int_{a_{i}}\omega\int_{b_{i}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{b_{i}}\omega\int_{a_{i}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}}\omega\int_{d_{i}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

Note that the last term does not depend on the orientation of
d_{i}
.

Theorem 5.1

(
Change of separating family
)
.

Suppose
\int_{c}\omega\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for any circle
c
on
\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
. For two separating families
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
and
x_{0}\in\Sigma\setminus({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime})
, we have

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\in\begin{cases}\pi{\mathbb{Z}},&\Sigma\textrm{ has no corners},\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\pi{\mathbb{Z}},&\textrm{in general}.\end{cases}

In particular, this holds for admissible
1
-forms.

Proof.

Recall the Gauss–Bonnet formula: For a compact Riemannian surface
(\Omega,g)
with piecewise smooth boundary,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Omega}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\sum\alpha=2\pi\chi(\Omega),

where
\sum\alpha
is the sum of the turning angles along
\partial\Omega
.

We first study the effect of changing the
d_{i}
. It suffices to consider the case of changing one
d_{i}
, i.e., suppose that
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
differs from
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
only in
d_{i}
. We add a prime
′
to denote the corresponding object for
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
. For notational convenience, suppose
i=1
and
d_{1}
goes from
c_{1}
to
c_{2}
.

Step 1: Changing
d_{1}
to a homologous
d_{1}^{\prime}
.

First suppose
d_{1}^{\prime\circ}\cap d_{1}^{\circ}=\varnothing
. By (3) of
2.2
, let
\Omega
be the connected component of
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup d_{1}^{\prime})
not containing
x_{0}
. For
i=1,2
, let
y_{i}=d_{1}\cap c_{i}
,
y_{i}^{\prime}=d_{1}^{\prime}\cap c_{i}
, and let
s_{i}
be the segment
\partial\Omega\cap c_{i}
on
c_{i}
from
y_{i}
to
y_{i}^{\prime}
. (If
y_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}
,
s_{i}
is degenerate.) Without loss of generality, suppose the orientations of
d_{1}^{\prime}
,
s_{1}
coincide with those induced by
\Omega
, so that the orientations of
d_{1}
,
s_{2}
are reverse to those induced by
\Omega
. See
Fig.

9
for a possible situation.

By definition,
I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
except on
\Omega
, where
I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)+\int_{e_{1}}\omega
, so

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(\int_{s_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{2}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\sum_{c\in C_{\Omega}}\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right),

where
C_{\Omega}=\{c\in C\setminus\{z_{i}\}_{i}:c\subset\partial\Omega\}
. Since the
e_{i}
are unchanged,

\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}^{\prime}}\omega\int_{d_{i}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}}\omega\int_{d_{i}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(\int_{d_{1}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right).

By the Gauss–Bonnet formula on
\Omega
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{s_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{d_{1}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{2}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}+\sum_{c\in C_{\Omega}}\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=2\pi\chi(\Omega)-\sum\alpha.

Note that the terms for the
d_{i}
contained in
\partial\Omega
cancel out because
\partial\Omega
traverses them twice with opposite directions. Summing up, we get

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(2\pi\chi(\Omega)-\sum\alpha\right).

Since
\int_{e_{1}}\omega\in{\mathbb{Z}}
by assumption and
\chi(\Omega)\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, it remains to count
\sum\alpha
:

•

The angles at
y_{1}
,
y_{1}^{\prime}
,
y_{2}
,
y_{2}^{\prime}
contribute
2\pi
, regardless of whether
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
are degenerate.

•

There may be right angles on
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
or the circles in
C_{\Omega}
coming from the corners of
\Sigma
, which contribute an integral multiple of
\pi/2
. If
\partial_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, this part does not exist.

•

The ends of the
d_{i}
contained in
\partial\Omega
contribute an integral multiple of
\pi
: An end in a boundary circle contributes two right angles. At puncture ends, all the turning angles are
\pi
, since the tangent vectors of the
d_{i}
are fixed.

Thus
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\in\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\partial_{\mathrm{M}}\Sigma=\varnothing
,
\frac{1}{2}\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
in general.

Figure 9
:
Changing
d_{1}
to a disjoint homologous
d_{1}^{\prime}

For the general case, we use the following argument. First, for
d_{1}^{\prime}
close to
d_{1}
, it is easy to find
d_{1}^{\prime\prime}
with
d_{1}^{\prime\prime\circ}\cap d_{1}^{\circ}=d_{1}^{\prime\prime\circ}\cap d_{1}^{\prime\circ}=\varnothing
, so that we can apply the above to the pairs
d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
d_{1}^{\prime},d_{1}^{\prime\prime}
to get the result. This allows us to perturb
d_{1}
,
d_{1}^{\prime}
. In particular, in the general case, we may assume that
d_{1}
,
d_{1}^{\prime}
intersect transversally. Then they intersect at finitely many points, giving a finite number of regions on which we apply the Gauss–Bonnet formula. Summing up, the angles at the intersections in
d_{1}^{\circ}\cap d_{1}^{\prime\circ}
cancel out or give
\pi
, so the conclusion is the same.

Step 2: Changing
d_{1}
to a nonhomologous
d_{1}^{\prime}
.

It suffices to consider the case of changing an end of
d_{1}
to a neighboring element in
C
. For notational convenience, suppose
d_{2}
goes from
c_{2}
to
c_{3}
and
d_{1}^{\prime}
goes from
c_{1}
to
c_{3}
. Again, consider the case
d_{1}^{\circ}\cap d_{1}^{\prime\circ}=\varnothing
for simplicity. As in the previous step, let
\Omega
be the connected component of
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup d_{1}^{\prime})
not containing
x_{0}
. Let
y_{1}=d_{1}\cap c_{1}
,
y_{2}=d_{1}\cap c_{2}
,
y_{2}^{\prime}=d_{2}\cap c_{2}
,
y_{3}=d_{2}\cap c_{3}
,
y_{1}^{\prime}=d_{1}^{\prime}\cap c_{1}
,
y_{3}^{\prime}=d_{1}^{\prime}\cap c_{3}
. For
i=1,2,3
, let
s_{i}
be the segment
\partial\Omega\cap c_{i}
on
c_{i}
from
y_{i}
to
y_{i}^{\prime}
, which may be degenerate. Without loss of generality, suppose the orientations of
d_{1}^{\prime}
,
s_{1}
coincide with those induced by
\Omega
, so that the orientations of
d_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
d_{2}
,
s_{3}
are reverse to those induced by
\Omega
. See
Fig.

10
for a possible situation.

Again,
I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
except on
\Omega
, where
I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)+\int_{e_{1}}\omega
, so

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(\int_{s_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{2}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{3}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\sum_{c\in C_{\Omega}}\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right),

where
C_{\Omega}=\{c\in C\setminus\{z_{i}\}_{i}:c\subset\partial\Omega\}
. Now the
e_{i}
change. More precisely, we have the following relations. Deleting
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
from the tree formed by the
d_{i}
, it splits into three connected components. For
i=1,2,3
, let
C_{i}
be the set of elements in
C
in the connected component of
c_{i}
, so that
C=C_{1}\sqcup C_{2}\sqcup C_{3}
. Then
[e_{1}]=\sum_{c\in C_{1}}[c]
,
[e_{2}]=\sum_{c\in C_{1}\sqcup C_{2}}[c]=-\sum_{c\in C_{3}}[c]
,
[e_{1}^{\prime}]=\sum_{c\in C_{1}}[c]=[e_{1}]
,
[e_{2}^{\prime}]=\sum_{c\in C_{2}}[c]=[e_{2}]-[e_{1}]
, and the other
e_{i}
are unchanged. Thus

\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}^{\prime}}\omega\int_{d_{i}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}}\omega\int_{d_{i}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(\int_{d_{1}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{2}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right).

By the Gauss–Bonnet formula on
\Omega
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{s_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{d_{1}^{\prime}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{3}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{2}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}-\int_{s_{2}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}}k_{g}\,\ell_{g}+\sum_{c\in C_{\Omega}}\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=2\pi\chi(\Omega)-\sum\alpha.

Summing up, we get

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\int_{e_{1}}\omega\left(2\pi\chi(\Omega)-\sum\alpha\right).

The angles are counted similarly, the main difference being that the angles at
y_{1}
,
y_{1}^{\prime}
,
y_{2}
,
y_{2}^{\prime}
,
y_{3}
,
y_{3}^{\prime}
contribute
3\pi
.

Figure 10
:
Changing
d_{1}
to a disjoint nonhomologous
d_{1}^{\prime}

Step 3: The general case.

We turn to the general case. Our goal is to reduce to the case of closed surfaces, which was treated in detail in
[
32
, Appendix A]
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
be separating families of
\Sigma
.

Removing a small triangular region at each corner of
\Sigma
as in
Fig.

11(a)
, we obtain a compact surface without corners
\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime})\cap({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cup{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}\cup x_{0})=\varnothing
. We view
\Sigma^{\prime}
as an extended surface with Dirichlet boundary, so that
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
are also separating families of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The point is that the theorem for
\Sigma
is equivalent to the theorem for
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Indeed, since the regularization terms are the same for
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
, we have

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial(\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime})}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

and likewise for
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
. Thus

\displaystyle(K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega))-(K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}}K_{g}(I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial(\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime})}k_{g}(I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

Now, on each connected component of
\Sigma^{\circ}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
, i.e., on the removed region bounded by
\partial\Sigma
and
\partial\Sigma^{\prime}
at a corner of
\Sigma
,
I_{x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
is constant with value given by a cycle of
\omega
, which is in
{\mathbb{Z}}
by assumption. We take it out of the integral and apply the Gauss–Bonnet formula to get
(K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega))-(K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))=(K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))-(K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))\in\frac{1}{2}\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus we may assume that
\Sigma
has no corners.

In the same vein, one observes that the theorem is invariant after removing a small disk around each puncture
z_{i}
(such that the boundary of the disk is orthogonal to the
d_{j}
incident to
z_{i}
, see
Fig.

11(b)
). Thus we may assume that
\Sigma
has no punctures.

(a)

Eliminating a corner

(b)

Eliminating a puncture

Figure 11
:
Reducing to the case of closed surfaces

Now suppose
\Sigma
has no corners or punctures. We use the same argument as in
[
32
, Lemma 8.3]
to remove the boundary. We label the boundary
\partial\Sigma
as Neumann. By
5.2
below, we can take
g
Neumann extendible and
x_{0}\in\partial\Sigma
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=(a_{i},b_{i})_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{g}}}\times(d_{j})_{j=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}
be a separating family of
\Sigma
. By Step 2, we may change the
d_{j}
so that
d_{j}
goes from
c_{j}
to
c_{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}}
for all
j
. Consider the doubling
\Sigma^{\#2}
of
\Sigma
and the reflection
\tau
on
\Sigma^{\#2}
. For all
j
,
d_{j}^{\#2}=d_{j}\cup\tau(d_{j})
glues to a simple circle on
\Sigma^{\#2}
, and it is easy to see that
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\#2}=(a_{i},b_{i},\tau(b_{i}),\tau(a_{i}))_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{g}}\times(d_{j}^{\#2},c_{j})_{j=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}
is a separating family (i.e., interior topological basis) of
\Sigma^{\#2}
, and
K_{\Sigma^{\#2},g^{\#2},x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\#2}}(\omega^{\#2})=\frac{1}{2}K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)
. One does the same with any other separating family
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
to get
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime\#2}
, so
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\big(K_{\Sigma^{\#2},g^{\#2},x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime\#2}}(\omega^{\#2})-K_{\Sigma^{\#2},g^{\#2},x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\#2}}(\omega^{\#2})\big)\in\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
by
[
32
, Appendix A]
.
∎

Remark
.

Recall that we have a different convention for the
d_{i}
in a separating family, which results in a different anomaly here. In the convention of
[
32
]
, the anomaly is in
2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
. In our convention, the anomaly is at best in
\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
, even for closed surfaces. We remark also that an anomaly in
\frac{\pi}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
seems inevitable in the presence of corners.

The following are direct calculations:

Proposition 5.2
.

The following hold:

(1)

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
is linear in
\omega
.

(2)

For
f\in C^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{1}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(df)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}f\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-(2\pi\chi(\Sigma)-\pi{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}})f(x_{0}).

(3)

(
Change of base point
)
For
x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\in\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
,

K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=(2\pi\chi(\Sigma)-\pi{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}})\int_{\gamma}\omega,

where
\gamma
is any path on
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
from
x_{0}
to
x_{0}^{\prime}
.

(4)

(
Change of conformal metric
)
For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,

K_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\langle d\rho,\omega\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma
has
2{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}
corners, the Gauss–Bonnet formula on
\Sigma
reads

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=2\pi\chi(\Sigma)-\pi{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}.

(1-3) are clear. As for (4), recall that

K_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{-\rho}(K_{g}+\Delta_{g}\rho),\qquad{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{\rho}\;\!{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},\qquad k_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\rho}(k_{g}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\rho),\qquad{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{e^{\rho}g}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\rho}\;\!{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}K_{e^{\rho}g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}(K_{g}+\Delta_{g}\rho)I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{e^{\rho}g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{e^{\rho}g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}(k_{g}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\rho)I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

By integration by parts on
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
,
6
6

6

We are really doing integration by parts on
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
from the proof of
2.2
.

\int_{\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\Delta_{g}\rho\,I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=\int_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\circ}}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,(I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{+}-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{-})\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{\Sigma\setminus\sigma}\langle d\rho,\omega\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

where
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{\pm}
is the limit of
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
as one approaches from the right (resp. left). By definition,
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{+}-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{-}
is equal to
\int_{b_{i}}\omega
on
a_{i}
,
-\int_{a_{i}}\omega
on
b_{i}
,
-\int_{e_{i}}\omega
on
d_{i}
. Thus

\int_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\circ}}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,(I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{+}-I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)^{-})\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\int_{a_{i}}\omega\int_{b_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{b_{i}}\omega\int_{a_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{{\mathfrak{b}}}-1}\int_{e_{i}}\omega\int_{d_{i}}\partial_{\nu}\rho\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

Summing up, we get the desired formula.
∎

Proposition 5.3

(
Change under gluing
)
.

We resume the notations in
Section

2.7
.

•

In the case of gluing two surfaces, let
g
be a conformal metric on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
and
x_{0}
a point on the glued (semi)circle. For a closed
1
-form
\omega
on
\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime}
,
K_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=K_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)+K_{\Sigma^{\prime},g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}},x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)
.

•

In the case of self-gluing a surface, let
g
be a conformal metric on
\#\Sigma
and
x_{0}
a point
not
on the glued (semi)circle. For a closed
1
-form
\omega
on
\#\Sigma
,
K_{\#\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)=K_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
except in case 1 of the case of gluing along a semicircle, where
K_{\#\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\in(\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega)\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

The only nontrivial case is case 1 of the case of gluing along a semicircle (
Fig.

7
), where we moved
{\mathcal{C}}
to
c\not\subset\bigcup{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
. We have
I_{x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
except on the region
\Omega
bounded by
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}
and
c
, where
I_{x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)=I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega
. Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\#\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}

\displaystyle=-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g},

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\#\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\#{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\left(\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{c_{1}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}k_{g}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{N}}}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M_{D}}}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

The difference between the regularization terms is

\int_{c}\omega\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}-\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}}\omega\int_{d_{1}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=-\int_{d_{1}^{\#}}\omega\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}.

By the Gauss–Bonnet formula on
\Omega
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}K_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{c}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}+\int_{c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}}k_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=-k_{2}\pi,

where
k_{2}
is the number of Dirichlet (or Neumann) semicircles on
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}
, so that
c_{2}^{\mathrm{M}}
has
2k_{2}
corners. Summing up, we get the desired formula.
∎

Finally, we discuss the dependence on
{\mathbf{v}}
, which we have implicitly fixed up to this point. We view the tangent bundle of
\Sigma
as a complex line bundle, so that
\lambda v
is defined for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
v
a tangent vector on
\Sigma
. For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\mathfrak{s}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, we write
e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}}=(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{1}}v_{1},\ldots,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{\mathfrak{s}}}v_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Proposition 5.4

(
Change of tangent vectors
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
be a separating family with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
. For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in[0,2\pi)^{\mathfrak{s}}
, there exists a separating family
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
with respect to
e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}}
such that
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\theta_{i}m_{i}
for any
\omega
, where
m_{i}
is the winding number of
\omega
around
z_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
with
\chi|_{[0,1/3]}=1
,
\chi|_{[2/3,\infty)}=0
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
R_{\alpha}:re^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}\mapsto re^{{\mathrm{i}}(\theta+\alpha\chi(r))}
is a diffeomorphism of
{\mathbb{D}}
with
R_{\alpha}={\mathrm{id}}
near
\partial{\mathbb{D}}
,
R_{\alpha}=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha}\,{\mathrm{id}}
near
0
. For each
z_{i}
, let
\Psi_{i}:D_{i}\to{\mathbb{D}}
be a chart centered at
z_{i}
such that the
D_{i}
are disjoint and
x_{0}\not\in\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}D_{i}
. Consider the diffeomorphism
\Phi
of
\Sigma
defined to be
\Psi_{i}^{-1}R_{\theta_{i}}\Psi_{i}
on
D_{i}
and
{\mathrm{id}}
on
\Sigma\setminus\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}D_{i}
. Then
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is a separating family with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
if and only if
\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is a separating family with respect to
e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}}
. We claim that
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\theta_{i}\int_{c^{\mathrm{P}}_{i}}\omega
. If
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is such that
z_{i}\cap d_{j}\neq\varnothing
if and only if
i=j
, then this follows from a simple application of the Gauss–Bonnet formula on the region in each
D_{i}
bounded by
d_{i}
and
\Phi_{*}d_{i}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}
be another separating family with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
. Since
\Phi
preserves angles near
\partial\Sigma\cup{\mathbf{z}}
, it does not change the turning angles in the Gauss–Bonnet formulas in the proof of
5.1
, so
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)=K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)
, i.e.,
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}}(\omega)=K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\Phi_{*}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}(\omega)-K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
. Thus the claim holds for any separating family
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
.
∎

6
Imaginary Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos

In this section we focus on the GFF on
\Sigma
with at least one Neumann boundary, i.e.
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\not=\varnothing
. As the metric is assumed to be Neumann extendible, we have the doubled surface
\Sigma^{\#2}
with a doubled metric
g^{\#2}
. Moreover, recall
\tau
is the involution on
\Sigma^{\#2}
, we have the following relation between the green function
G_{g}^{\Sigma}
and
G_{g^{\#2}}^{\Sigma^{\#2}}

G^{\Sigma}_{g}(x,y)=G^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{g^{\#2}}(x,y)+G^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{g^{\#2}}(x,\tau(y)).

Similar to
[
32
, Section 5]
, based on certain regularized sequences
(X_{g,\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
, we can define the imaginary bulk (resp. boundary) Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC for short)
M_{g}({\mathrm{d}}v_{g})
(resp.
L_{g}({\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})
) as the following
L^{2}(\Omega)
limit (see
[
32
, Section 5.4]
e.g.)

M_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-\beta^{2}/2}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta X_{g,\epsilon}(x)}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x),\ \ \ L_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-\beta^{2}/4}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta/2X_{g,\epsilon}(x)}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x).

For some
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, we consider the metric
g^{\prime}:=e^{\rho}g
conformal to
g
. By comparing the covariances under two metrics, we see that

X_{g^{\prime}}\overset{d}{=}X_{g}-m_{g^{\prime}}(X_{g}),

where
m_{g}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{v_{g}(\Sigma)}\int\cdot{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
. Moreover, we have the following rules under change of metric.

Proposition 6.1
.

For
g^{\prime}:=e^{\rho}g
, we have the following change of metric formula for imaginary GMC:

M_{g^{\prime}}(X_{g^{\prime}},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g^{\prime}})\overset{d}{=}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta({\mathrm{i}}Q\rho/2-m_{g^{\prime}}(X_{g}))}M_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}),

and

L_{g^{\prime}}(X_{g^{\prime}},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g^{\prime}})\overset{d}{=}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta/2({\mathrm{i}}Q\rho/2-m_{g^{\prime}}(X_{g}))}L_{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}).

Assume first that
\Sigma
has at least one Dirichlet boundary (including mixed boundaries). Suppose
p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t}(x,y)
is the transition kernel (depending on
g^{\#2}
) of a speed 2 Brownian motion on
\Sigma^{\#2}
killed upon hitting the boundary. Then the covariance of
X_{g}
(or Green function) is written as

2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})+p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t}(x,\tau(x^{\prime})){\mathrm{d}}t,x,x^{\prime}\in\Sigma.

Furthermore, we have
X_{g,\epsilon}
the white noise cut off

X_{g,\epsilon}(x):=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma}p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t/2}(x,y)+p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t/2}(x,\tau(y))W({\mathrm{d}}t,{\mathrm{d}}y),

whose covariance is

\mathbb{E}[X_{g,\epsilon}(x)X_{g,\epsilon^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]=2\pi\int_{\epsilon^{2}\vee\epsilon^{\prime 2}}^{\infty}p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})+p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{t}(x,\tau(x^{\prime})){\mathrm{d}}t.

(2)

Proposition 6.2
.

Consider a measurable function
f:{\Sigma}\cup\partial\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
and an arbitrary real-valued random variable
Z
, for some constant
C
depending on
\beta
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left|\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}(X_{g}+Z,\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))\right|+\left|\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f_{g}(x)L(X_{g}+Z,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\right)\right]\\
\leq&e^{CV(f)}\sqrt{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(\Sigma,f)}e^{CU_{1}(\Sigma,f)}\right)\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f)}e^{CU_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f)}\right)},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}&U_{1}(\Sigma,f):=\iint_{\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|(d(x,y)^{-\beta^{2}}+d(x,\tau(y))^{-\beta^{2}}){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y),\\
&U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f):=\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|d(x,y)^{-\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y),\\
&V(f):=\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|d(x,\partial\Sigma)^{-\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}|f(x)|d(x,{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)^{-\beta^{2}/4}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x).\end{split}

(3)

Proof.

Similar to the proof in
[
32
, Proposition 5.3]
, we can throw away
Z
and only need to consider functions s.t.
\mathrm{Im}(f)=0
, i.e. we just need to prove the exponential moment for

\int_{\Sigma}\epsilon^{-\beta^{2}/2}f(x)\cos(\beta X_{g,\epsilon}(x)){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}\epsilon^{-\beta^{2}/4}f(x)\cos(\beta/2X_{g,\epsilon}(x)){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x).

(4)

Replacing
\epsilon
with
e^{-t}
, we have the following semi-martingale

\mathcal{S}_{t}:=\int_{\Sigma}e^{\beta^{2}t/2}f(x)\cos(\beta X_{g,e^{-t}}(x)){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}e^{\beta^{2}t/4}f(x)\cos(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-t}}(x)){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x),

whose limit when
t\to\infty
is the limit of (
4
) as
\epsilon\to 0
.

By the knowledge of Itô’s calculus, we have
{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{S}_{t}={\mathrm{d}}A_{t}+{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{M}_{t}
, where

\begin{split}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{M}_{t}:=&-\beta\int_{\Sigma}f(x)\sin(\beta X_{g,e^{-t}}(x))e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\mathrm{d}X_{g,e^{-t}}(x)\\
&-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f(x)\sin(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-t}}(x))e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\mathrm{d}X_{g,e^{-t}}(x),\end{split}

and

\begin{split}{\mathrm{d}}A_{t}:=&\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\int_{\Sigma}f(x)\cos(\beta X_{g,e^{-t}}(x))e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)({\mathrm{d}}t-\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-t}}(x)\rangle_{t})\\
+&\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f(x)\cos(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-t}}(x))e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)({\mathrm{d}}t-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-t}}(x)\rangle_{t}).\end{split}

The quadratic variation of the local martingale part is

\begin{split}\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle_{t}-\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle_{0}=\beta^{2}&\int_{0}^{t}\iint_{\Sigma^{2}}f(x)f(y)\sin(\beta X_{g,e^{-u}}(x))\sin(\beta X_{g,e^{-u}}(y))\\
&e^{{\beta^{2}u}}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-u}}(x),X_{g,e^{-u}}(y)\rangle_{u}\\
+\beta^{2}/4&\int_{0}^{t}\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma^{2}}f(x)f(y)\sin(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-u}}(x))\sin(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-u}}(y))\\
&e^{\frac{\beta^{2}u}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-u}}(x),X_{g,e^{-u}}(y)\rangle_{u}\\
+\beta^{2}&\int_{0}^{t}\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\times\Sigma}f(x)f(y)\sin(\beta X_{g,e^{-u}}(x))\sin(\beta/2X_{g,e^{-u}}(y))\\
&e^{\frac{3\beta^{2}u}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-u}}(x),X_{g,e^{-u}}(y)\rangle_{u}.\end{split}

Recall that we have (
2
), and taking derivative w.r.t.
u
, we get

\mathrm{d}\langle X_{g,e^{-u}}(x),X_{g,e^{-u}}(y)\rangle_{u}=4\pi e^{-2u}(p_{e^{-2u}}^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}(x,y)+p^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}_{e^{-2u}}(x,\tau(y)))\mathrm{d}u.

(5)

According to
[
16
]
, we have the following estimate for the Dirichlet heat kernel
p^{\Sigma^{\#2}}_{e^{-2u}}(x,y)

e^{-2u}p_{e^{-2u}}^{\Sigma^{\#2}}(x,y)\leq Ce^{-d(x,y)^{2}e^{2u}/C},

(6)

where
C
is a constant that may change from line to line. Combining everything and replacing all sine functions by 1, we see that

\begin{split}\sup_{t\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle_{t}-\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle_{0}&\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}\iint_{\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|e^{\beta^{2}t}(e^{-d(x,y)^{2}e^{2t}/C}+e^{-d(x,\tau(y))^{2}e^{2t}/C}){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}t\\
&+C\int_{0}^{\infty}\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|e^{\beta^{2}t/2}e^{-d(x,y)^{2}e^{2t}/C}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}t\\
&+C\int_{0}^{\infty}\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\times\Sigma}|f(x)f(y)|e^{3\beta^{2}t/4}e^{-d(x,y)^{2}e^{2t}/C}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Then we first integrate over time
t
(and perform the change of variables
s=d(x,y)e^{t}
or
s=d(x,\tau(y))e^{t}
), the above quantity becomes

\begin{split}&C\iint_{\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|(d(x,y)^{-\beta^{2}}+d(x,\tau(y))^{-\beta^{2}}){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)\\
&+C\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma^{2}}|f(x)f(y)|d(x,y)^{-\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\\
&+C\iint_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma\times\Sigma}|f(x)f(y)|d(x,y)^{-3\beta^{2}/4}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y).\end{split}

(7)

Now we are back to
\mathcal{M}_{t}^{0}:=\mathcal{M}_{t}-\mathcal{M}_{0}
. By Fatou’s lemma

\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu\mathcal{M}^{0}_{\infty}}]\leq e^{\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\sup_{t\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{M}^{0}\rangle_{t}},

because
\mathcal{M}^{0}
is a martingale. And by Chernoff’s bound, it is easy to see that

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{M}^{0}_{\infty}|\geq x)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{4C\sup_{t\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{M}^{0}\rangle_{t}}\right).

Using Fubini’s theorem, we have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\mathcal{|}\mathcal{M}^{0}_{\infty}|}]=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{M}^{0}_{\infty}|\geq x)e^{x}\mathrm{d}x\leq 1+C\sqrt{\sup_{t\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{M}^{0}\rangle_{t}}\ e^{C\sup_{t\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{M}^{0}\rangle_{t}}.

The
U_{1}(\Sigma,f),U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f)
in the Proposition
6.2
is the bulk and boundary part of
\langle\mathcal{M}^{0}\rangle_{t}
respectively. Note that we can avoid the cross term in the last line of (
7
) if we deal with the bulk and the boundary separately. Indeed, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left|\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M(X_{g}+Z,\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))\right|+\left|\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f(x)L(X_{g}+Z,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\right)\right]\\
\leq&\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(2\left|\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M(X_{g}+Z,\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))\right|\right)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(2\left|\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f(x)L(X_{g}+Z,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\right)\right]}.\end{split}

On the other hand, using the expression of (
5
), we get that

\begin{split}|A_{\infty}-A_{0}|\leq&\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{2}}|1-4\pi e^{-2u}(p_{e^{-2u}}^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}(x,x)+p^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}_{e^{-2u}}(x,\tau(x)))|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\\
+&\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}|f(x)|e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{4}}|1-4\pi e^{-2u}p_{e^{-2u}}^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}(x,x)|{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\end{split}

(8)

The first line in the above equation can be further bounded by

\begin{split}&\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{2}}|1-4\pi e^{-2u}p_{e^{-2u}}^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}(x,x)|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\\
+&\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|e^{\frac{\beta^{2}t}{2}}|4\pi e^{-2u}p^{{\Sigma^{\#2}}}_{e^{-2u}}(x,\tau(x))|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}t,\end{split}

(9)

where, according to (
6
), the second term is readily controlled by
\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|d(x,\tau(x))^{-\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)
. The remaining parts of
|A_{\infty}-A_{0}|
require the following estimate of the heat kernel by
[
51
]

|4\pi e^{-2u}p_{e^{-2u}}^{\Sigma^{\#2}}(x,x)-1|\leq C(e^{-2u}+e^{-d(x,{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)^{2}e^{2u}/C}),

which is true for some
C>0
and for all
u\in(0,\infty)
. Thus, plugging the estimate into (
8
) and (
9
), we have

|A_{\infty}-A_{0}|\leq C\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|d(x,\partial\Sigma)^{-\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)+C\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}|f(x)|d(x,{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma)^{-\beta^{2}/4}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x).

Finally, we only need to control
|\mathcal{S}_{0}|=|\mathcal{M}_{0}+A_{0}|
, which is just

|\mathcal{S}_{0}|\leq C(\int_{\Sigma}|f(x)|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}|f(x)|{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)).

This part is bounded by the estimate for
|A_{\infty}-A_{0}|
, which corresponds to
V(f)
.
∎

The renormalization of the bulk GMC produces singularities on the Dirichlet boundaries of
\Sigma
(but still has an exponential moment), as is stressed in
[
32
, Proposition 5.2]
. Now, we would focus on surfaces with only Neumann boundaries, but we have to create a Dirichlet boundary (so that we have the white noise decomposition of GFF) and remove it later.

Specifically, we cut the surface
\Sigma
using three geodesic circles in its interior, which would be helpful later. Abusively, let
d(\cdot,\cdot)
be the distance induced by the metric
g
or
g^{\#2}
. Denote by
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
and
D_{3}
three geodesic closed balls s.t.

•

D_{1}\subset D_{2}\subset D_{3}
and
d(D_{1},\partial D_{2})>0,d(D_{2},\partial D_{3})>0
, i.e.
D_{i}
is strictly contained in
D_{i+1}
;

•

d(D_{3},\partial\Sigma)>0
, i.e.
D_{3}
stays away from the boundary of
\Sigma
.

For each of these balls, according to the domain Markov property of a GFF (see
[
32
, Section 5.3]
e.g.) on
\Sigma
, we can decompose
X_{g}
into three independent parts

X^{\Sigma\backslash D_{i}}_{g}+X^{D_{i}}_{g}+P_{i},i=1,2,3,

where

•

X^{\Sigma\backslash D_{i}}_{g}
is a GFF with Neumann conditions on
\partial\Sigma
and Dirichlet condition on
\partial D_{i}
;

•

X^{D_{i}}_{g}
is a GFF with Dirichlet condition on
\partial D_{i}
;

•

P
is the sum of a harmonic extension of
X|_{\partial D_{i}}
onto
\Sigma
satisfying Neumann condition on
\partial\Sigma
and a Gaussian random variable.

Let
A_{i}:={\Sigma\backslash D_{i}}
. Then using the above proposition and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

Corollary 6.3
.

Consider a measurable function
f:\Sigma\cup\partial\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
and an arbitrary real-valued random variable
Z
, for some constant
C
depending on
\beta
and the way we choose
D_{1},D_{2}
and
D_{3}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left|\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M(X_{g}+Z,\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))\right|+\left|\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}f(x)L(X_{g}+Z,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\right)\right]\\
\leq&e^{CV(f)}\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(D_{2},f)}e^{CU_{1}(D_{2},f)}\right)\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(A_{2},f)}e^{CU_{1}(A_{2},f)}\right)}\\
&\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f)}e^{CU_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,f)}\right)},\end{split}

where
U_{1},U_{2},V
are the same as the one in Proposition
6.2
.

Finally, we prove the Imaginary version of Cameron-Martin theorem for imaginary GMC on an arbitrary surface.

Proposition 6.4
.

Consider a bounded functional
F
of GFF
X_{g}
s.t.
F(X_{g}+wY)
has an analytic continuation in
w
for the field
Y:=2\pi\int G_{g}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}m(y)
, where
m
is an arbitrary measure s.t.
Y
is continuous on
\Sigma\cup\partial\Sigma
.
f(x)
is a bounded measurable function on
\Sigma\cup\partial\Sigma
and
Z
is a random variable. Then we have for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Bigg[F(X_{g})\exp\Bigg({\mathrm{i}}\alpha\int X{\mathrm{d}}m+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\int 2\pi G_{g}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}m(x){\mathrm{d}}m(y)+\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}(x))\Bigg)\Bigg]\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha Y\right)\exp\left(\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha Y,\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x)\right)+\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha Y,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}(x)\right)\right)\right].\end{split}

Proof.

We introduce the function

\begin{split}A(w):=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[F(X_{g})\exp\Bigg(&w\int X{\mathrm{d}}m-\frac{w^{2}}{2}\int 2\pi G_{g}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}m(x){\mathrm{d}}m(y)\\
&+\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},\mathrm{d}v_{g}(x))+\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}(x))\Bigg)\Bigg],\end{split}

which is holomorphic for
w\in\mathbb{C}
. When
w\in{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}
, we can apply Cameron-Martin theorem to
A(w)
and obtain

A(w)=\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY\right)\exp\left(\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)+\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)\right)\right].

(10)

We want to show that (
10
) is holomorphic as well, so as to apply analytic continuation to
A(w)
before and after the shift by Cameron-Martin theorem. Hence we will need to approximate
\int f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g}+iwY,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))
and
\int f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g}+iwY,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))
by holomorphic functions. Note that the definition of
F
is important to the proof. To start with, we claim they are Schwarz distributions with order 2 before the shift.

Lemma 6.5
.

f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))
and
f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))
are two random (Schwarz) distributions with order 2 on
\Sigma
. Moreover, there exists a
L^{2}
random variable
D_{\Sigma}
such that for a smooth function
h
on
\bar{\Sigma}
,

\left|\int_{\Sigma}h(x)f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))+\int_{\partial\Sigma}h(x)f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\leq D_{\Sigma}(\sup_{\bar{\Sigma}}|h|+\sup_{\Sigma}|\Delta_{g}h|+\sup_{\partial\Sigma}|\partial_{\nu}h|).

Proof.

By Green’s identity,

\int_{\Sigma}G_{g}(x,y)\Delta_{g}h(y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)-h(x)+\frac{1}{v_{g}(\Sigma)}\int_{\Sigma}h(y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)=\int_{\partial\Sigma}G(x,y)\partial_{\nu}h(y){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y).

Hence the smooth function
h
can be written as

h(x)=\frac{1}{v_{g}(\Sigma)}\int_{\Sigma}h(y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)+\int_{\Sigma}G_{g}(x,y)\Delta_{g}h(y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)-\int_{\partial\Sigma}G_{g}(x,y)\partial_{\nu}h(y){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)
. Similar to
[
32
, Lemma 6.12]
, we have

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{\Sigma}h(x)f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))+\int_{\partial\Sigma}h(x)f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\leq(\sup_{\bar{\Sigma}}|h|+\sup_{\Sigma}|\Delta_{g}h|+\sup_{\partial\Sigma}|\partial_{\nu}h|)\\
&\bigg(\left|\int f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))\right|+\left|\int f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|\\
+&\int\left|\int G_{g}(x,y)f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))\right|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)+\int\left|\int G_{g}(x,y)f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)\\
&+\int\left|\int G_{g}(x,y)f(x)M_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))\right|{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)+\int\left|\int G_{g}(x,y)f(x)L_{g}(Z+X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x))\right|{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y)\bigg).\end{split}

We only need to check that

D_{\Sigma}:=|M|+|L|+\int|\int Gf{\mathrm{d}}M|{\mathrm{d}}v+\int|\int Gf{\mathrm{d}}L|{\mathrm{d}}v+\int|\int Gf{\mathrm{d}}M|{\mathrm{d}}\ell+\int|\int Gf\mathrm{d}L|{\mathrm{d}}\ell

has a finite second moment. And by Jensen’s inequality, it is easy to see that we only need to control

|\int Gf\mathrm{d}M|^{2}+|\int Gf\mathrm{d}L|^{2},

which amounts to control locally

\int_{\mathbb{D}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\log\frac{1}{|x^{\prime}-y|}|x-x^{\prime}|^{-\beta^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\log\frac{1}{|x^{\prime}-y|}|x-x^{\prime}|^{-\beta^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}<\infty.

Since
\beta^{2}<2
, this integral over disk and interval is finite for sure.
∎

Next, we mollify
Y
with some smooth function
u_{\delta}
, i.e. by the convolution
Y*u_{\delta}(x):=\int_{\Sigma}u_{\delta}(x-y)Y(y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)
. we require that
\int_{\Sigma}u_{\delta}(x-y){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)\equiv 1
for all
x\in\bar{\Sigma}
. Since
Y
is continuous on
\bar{\Sigma}
, we can easily find a sequence of
(u_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
s.t.
\sup_{\bar{\Sigma}}|Y-Y*u_{\delta}|\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.

Then we replace the
M_{g}(X_{g}+wY,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))
(
L
resp.) in (
10
) by
M_{g}(X_{g}+wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x))
(
L
resp.), and the latter ones are holomorphic as a consequence of Lemma
6.5
(which justifies the differentiations by Lebesgue convergence theorem). We see that

\mathbb{E}\left[F\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta}\right)\exp\left(\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)+\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)\right)\right]

(11)

is holomorphic for any fixed
\delta>0
, since according to Corollary
6.3
, the above quantity is uniformly bounded on any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{C}
.
Now to prove (
10
) is holomorphic, we only need to show that (
11
) converges locally uniformly w.r.t.
w
to (
10
) when
\delta\to 0
. It is enough to prove that when
\delta\to 0
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg|\exp\bigg(&\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)-\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)\\
+&\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)-\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)\bigg)-1\bigg|^{2}\bigg]\end{split}

(12)

goes to zero as well. By Corollary
6.3
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg(&\bigg|\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)-\int_{\Sigma}f(x)M_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x)\right)\bigg|\\
+&\bigg|\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)-\int_{\partial\Sigma}f(x)L_{g}\left(Z+X_{g}+{\mathrm{i}}wY*u_{\delta},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x)\right)\bigg|\bigg)\bigg]\\
\leq&e^{CV(\varrho_{1})}\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(D,\varrho_{2})}e^{CU_{1}(D,\varrho_{2})}\right)\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D,\varrho_{2})}e^{CU_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D,\varrho_{2})}\right)}\\
&\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,\varrho_{3})}e^{CU_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,\varrho_{3})}\right)},\end{split}

where
\varrho_{2}:=f(e^{-\beta wY}-e^{-\beta wY*u_{\delta}}),\varrho_{3}:=f(e^{-\beta wY/2}-e^{-\beta wY*u_{\delta}/2})
and
\varrho_{1}:=\max(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{3})
goes to 1 as
\delta\to 0
locally uniformly in
w
. Hence (
12
) converges to zero, and (
10
) is holomorphic indeed.
∎

Remark
.

Proposition
6.4
can be generalized to multiple fields
Y_{1},Y_{2},\cdots
by induction. Indeed, consider
Y_{1}:=\int 2\pi G_{g}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}m_{1}(y)
, then
|e^{-\beta\alpha_{1}Y_{1}}|
is bounded for fixed
\alpha_{1}
and
F(X_{g}+i\alpha_{1}Y_{1})
is again a desirable functional. The shift from
Y_{1}
to
\int X{\mathrm{d}}m_{2}
is
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}\int{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}Y_{1}{\mathrm{d}}m_{2}}=e^{-\mathbb{E}[\alpha_{1}\int X{\mathrm{d}}m_{1}\alpha_{2}\int X{\mathrm{d}}m_{2}]}
.

7
BCILT path integrals

In this section, we define the path integrals in BCILT and verify that they define a CFT satisfying Segal’s axioms.

7.1
Correlation functions

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
g
a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
. Fix
{\mathbf{v}}
as in
2.1
. We define the following functionals on the Liouville field
\phi_{g}=[(X_{g},c+\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)})]
on
\Sigma
. More precisely, we define them on
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}\times{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})\times H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})
and push forward to
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
as in
3.7
. Then well-definedness amounts to checking that they do not depend on the choices of
x_{0},\omega
.

•

For the curvature terms

\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}\phi_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}\phi_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

we define (recall
Section

5
)

K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})=e^{{\mathrm{i}}Q\chi(\Sigma)c}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}QRK_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)}\,e^{\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}},

where
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is any separating family of
\Sigma
with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
disjoint from
x_{0}
. If
QR\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
, this is well-defined by
5.1
and
5.2
. (Here
\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}=0
since
\partial\Sigma
is necessarily geodesic.)

•

For the potential terms

\mu\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta\phi_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}\phi_{g}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

we define (recall
Section

6
)

\displaystyle M_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})

\displaystyle=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta(c+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}M_{g}^{\beta}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}),

\displaystyle L_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})

\displaystyle=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(c+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}L_{g}^{\beta}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}),

and

M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})=\mu\int_{\Sigma}M_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,L_{g}^{\beta}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}).

If
\beta R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
(or
{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\mu_{\partial}=0
), this is well-defined.

•

For the electric operators
