\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}\phi_{g}(z_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\phi_{g}(x_{j})},

it is necessary to regularize, since
\phi_{g}
is not defined pointwise. For
\varepsilon>0
, we introduce the averaging operator

(T_{\varepsilon}f)(z)=\frac{1}{\ell_{g}(\partial D_{g}(z,\varepsilon))}\int_{\partial D_{g}(z,\varepsilon)}f\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g},

where
D_{g}(z,r)
denotes the geodesic disk of radius
r
centered at
z
with respect to
g
. For the GFF
X_{g}
, we write
X_{g,\varepsilon}=T_{\varepsilon}X_{g}
. At each
z_{j}
, for
\alpha_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}c}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})},

where
I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})=\int_{\gamma_{j}}2\pi R\omega
,
\gamma_{j}
is a path on
\Sigma
from
x_{0}
to
z_{j}
such that
\gamma_{j}^{\circ}\subset\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}}
and the derivative of
\gamma_{j}
at
z_{j}
is
v_{j}
. If
\alpha_{j}R\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, this is well-defined up to
o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Indeed, changing the representative
\omega
to
\omega+\frac{1}{2\pi R}df
amounts to translating
X_{g}
by
h=f-\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}f\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
(as in the proof of
3.7
), which gives a multiplicative factor
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}(h(z_{j})-T_{\varepsilon}h(z_{j}))}=1+o(\varepsilon)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus it will be clear that the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
is well-defined. Similarly, at each
x_{j}
, for
\eta_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{4}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}c}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})}.

If
\eta_{j}R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
, this is well-defined up to
o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. To simplify the notation, we write

V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})=\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j}).

Remark
.

It is possible to define
V_{g,\varepsilon}
as an exactly well-defined (i.e., not just up to
o(1)
) function on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
, so that the integral below is well-defined for a fixed
\varepsilon
. One way to do this is as follows. Fix a separating family
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
of
\Sigma
with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
that is disjoint from
x_{0}
and
{\mathbf{x}}
. We define

V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{4}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}c}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}T_{\varepsilon}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})},

where
I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(2\pi R\omega):\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is as in
Section

5
, and similarly for
V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})
. Then
V_{g,\varepsilon}
is really invariant under changes of the representatives
\omega
. Moreover, this definition has the advantage that
V_{g,\varepsilon}
is well-defined as long as
\sum\alpha_{j}+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R
(rather than requiring that
\alpha_{j},\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R
for each
j
). Indeed, with this definition, the only term in
V_{g,\varepsilon}
that is only defined up to
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
is
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\big(\sum\alpha_{j}+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\big)c}
, and this makes sense if
\sum\alpha_{j}+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/R
. Intuitively, the reason is that the choice of a separating family allows us to relate the value of
I_{x_{0}}(\omega)
at different points, so that we have a global ambiguity in
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
instead of one for each
z_{j}
.

This definition of
V_{g,\varepsilon}
depends on the choice of a separating family
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
. However, since
(T_{\varepsilon}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(2\pi R\omega))(z_{j})\to I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})
(resp.
(T_{\varepsilon}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(2\pi R\omega))(x_{j})\to I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})
), the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
is well-defined and coincides with the limit for the previous definition of
V_{g,\varepsilon}
. In practice, it does not matter which definition we use after taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. Note that here our convention in
2.1
on the direction of the
d_{j}
is important. In the convention of
[
32
]
, it is not generally true that
(T_{\varepsilon}I_{x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(2\pi R\omega))(z_{j})\to I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})
.

Definition 7.1
.

For a measurable function
F:{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the
BCILT correlation function
of
F
is

\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}F(\phi_{g})\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}.

We define the following function class on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
be the linear space spanned by functions
F:{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
whose pullback via the homeomorphism in
3.7
has the form

F([(X_{g},c+\overline{I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)})])=e^{{\mathrm{i}}nc/R}\,P(\langle X_{g},h_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle X_{g},h_{m}\rangle)\,G([\omega]),

(13)

where
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
P
is a complex polynomial,
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
,
G:H_{\mathbf{m}}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a bounded function. This space is well-defined, i.e., it does not depend on the choices of
x_{0},\omega
. Note that
1\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose
\forall\,j
,
\alpha_{j},\eta_{j}>Q
. For
F\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
,
\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}
exists.

Proof.

Unraveling the definitions, it is

\begin{split}&\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{vol}_{g}\Sigma}{\det^{\prime}\Delta_{g}}}\sum_{[\omega]\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})}e^{-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})-QRK_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))}\\
&\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\sum\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{2}-\sum\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{4}}\int_{0}^{2\pi R}e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}-Q\chi(\Sigma))c}\,{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[e^{-R\int_{\Sigma}X_{g}\,d^{*}\omega\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,F(\phi_{g})\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j}))}\\
&\hskip 110.00017pte^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,e^{-\mu\int_{\Sigma}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta(c+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}M_{\beta}^{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})-\int_{\partial\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(c+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}L_{\beta}^{g}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})}\bigg]{\mathrm{d}}c,\end{split}

(14)

where the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}
is over the GFF
X_{g}
. By (1) of
2.7
, we may assume
d^{*}\omega=0
.

According to Proposition
6.4
, the expectation in (
14
) can be written as

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[F(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon})e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}\right]\\
&\times e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})+\sum_{j}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})-\frac{Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})^{2}]},\end{split}

(15)

where

\begin{split}u_{\varepsilon}(x):=&{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{g}(x)X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})]+{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{j}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{g}(x)X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})]\\
-&\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}(y)E[X_{g}(x)X_{g}(y)]\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}E[X_{g}(x)X_{g}(y)]\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y).\end{split}

Let
X_{g,0}:=X_{g}
, we have
u_{\varepsilon}(x)
converge to
u_{0}(x)
pointwisely on
\Sigma\setminus({\mathbf{z}}\cup{\mathbf{x}})
.
We first study the
L^{p}
limit of the exponential of GMC.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose
0>\alpha_{j}>Q,0>\eta_{j}>Q
and consider
\phi_{g},u_{\varepsilon}
defined as above. Then for all
p>1
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[|e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}-e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)}|^{p}]=0,

uniformly over all
\omega
.

Proof.

First, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the problem is reduced to

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[|e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}-e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)}|^{p}]\\
\leq&\mathbb{E}[|e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)}|^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[|e^{-\mu(M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma))-\mu_{\partial}(L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)-L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma))}-1|^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

By using
|e^{z}-1|\leq|e^{|z|}-1|
and
(e^{u}-1)^{p}\leq C(e^{pu}-1),u\geq 0,p>1
, it is enough to study for
\lambda>0
,

\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda|M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)|+\lambda|L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)-L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)|}]

(16)

goes to zero. According to Corollary
6.3
, (
16
) is smaller than

\begin{split}e^{CV(\varrho_{1})}&\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(D,\varrho_{2})}e^{CU_{1}(D,\varrho_{2})}\right)\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D,\varrho_{2})}e^{CU_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D,\varrho_{2})}\right)}\\
&\sqrt[3]{\left(1+C\sqrt{U_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,\varrho_{3})}e^{CU_{2}({\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma,\varrho_{3})}\right)},\end{split}

where
U_{1},U_{2},V
are all defined in
3
and
\varrho_{2}:=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u_{\epsilon}}-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\beta u_{0}},\varrho_{3}:=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u_{\epsilon}/2}-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\beta u_{0}/2}
and
\varrho_{1}:=\max(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{3})
.

Now it is plain to see our bound has nothing to do with
\omega
. Thus we just need to prove
U_{1},U_{2}
and
V
all converge to zero, and we will dominate all
{\mathrm{i}}u_{\varepsilon}
as follows. We could forget about the curvature terms in
u_{\varepsilon}
since they do not depend on
\varepsilon
.

For the green function we have the following inequality

G_{g}(x,y)\leq-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|d_{g}(x,y)|-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|d_{g}(x,\tau(y))|+C,

where
C
is a constant independent of
x,y\in\bar{\Sigma}
. Then we have a bound for
{\mathrm{i}}u_{\varepsilon}
uniformly over all
x\in\bar{\Sigma}
and
\varepsilon
small (where the
C
might change)

{\mathrm{i}}u_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}(\log|d_{g}(x,z_{j})|+\log|d_{g}(x,\tau(z_{j}))|)+\sum_{j}\eta_{j}\log|d_{g}(x,x_{j})|+C=:{\mathrm{i}}u^{\prime}(x).

Therefore, to prove
U_{1},U_{2}
and
V
converge to zero we only need to show that
U_{1}(\cdot,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u^{\prime}}),U_{2}(\cdot,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u^{\prime}/2}),\\
V(\cdot,\max(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u^{\prime}},e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta u^{\prime}/2}))
are finite and thus dominate
U_{1}(\cdot,\varrho_{2}),U_{2}(\cdot,\varrho_{3}),V(\cdot,\varrho_{1})
.
The evaluations of
U_{1},U_{2}
are determined by the following basic integrals

\iint_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\alpha}|y|^{\beta\alpha}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}}},\iint_{(\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H})^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\eta}|y|^{\beta\eta}}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}}}+\frac{|x|^{\beta\eta}|y|^{\beta\eta}}{|x-\bar{y}|^{\beta^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\eta/2}|y|^{\beta\eta/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}/2}}.

which are finite if
\beta\alpha-\beta^{2}/2>-2,\beta\eta-\beta^{2}/2>-2,
and
\beta\eta/2-\beta^{2}/4>-1
, i.e.
\alpha>Q
and
\eta>Q
. For
V
, we need

\int_{\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}}|x|^{\beta\eta}d(x,[-1,1])^{-\beta^{2}/2}\ {\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}r^{\beta\eta}|r\sin\theta|^{-\beta^{2}/2}r\ {\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}\theta,

which is again
\eta>Q
.
∎

The
L^{2}
convergence of
M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)
(
L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)
resp.) can be proved similarly. Let
W(x):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[X_{g,\varepsilon}^{2}(x)]+\log\varepsilon
. We point out that

\mathbb{E}[|M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)|^{2}]=\iint_{\Sigma^{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta(u_{0}(x)-\bar{u}_{0}(y))}e^{\beta^{2}G(x,y)-\beta^{2}/2(W(x)+W(y))+{\mathrm{i}}\beta(I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x)-I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(y))}{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}v_{g}(y)

and

\mathbb{E}[|L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)|^{2}]=\iint_{\partial\Sigma^{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(u_{0}(x)-\bar{u}_{0}(y))}e^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}G(x,y)-\frac{\beta^{2}}{8}(W(x)+W(y))+{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x)-I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(y))}{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(x){\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}(y).

are finite. The control of these second moments is equivalent to the control of the following integrals

\iint_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\alpha}|y|^{\beta\alpha}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}}},\iint_{(\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H})^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\eta}|y|^{\beta\eta}d(x,\bar{x})^{\beta^{2}/2}d(y,\bar{y})^{\beta^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}}|x-\bar{y}|^{\beta^{2}}},\iint_{[-1,1]^{2}}\frac{|x|^{\beta\eta/2}|y|^{\beta\eta/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\beta^{2}/2}},

which are finite if
\beta\alpha-\beta^{2}/2>-2,\beta\eta-\beta^{2}/2>-2,
and
\beta\eta/2-\beta^{2}/4>-1
, i.e.
\alpha>Q
and
\eta>Q
. Note that
\sqrt{d(x,\tau(x))d(y,\tau(y))}\leq d(x,\tau(y))
.
Now we sketch the proof of convergence of

\begin{split}&\sum_{[\omega]\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})}e^{-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})-QRK_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))}\\
&\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{2\pi R}e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}-Q\chi(\Sigma))c}\,\mathbb{E}\left[F(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon})e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c,\end{split}

where
\varepsilon^{-\sum\alpha_{j}^{2}/2-\sum\eta_{j}^{2}/8}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})+\sum_{j}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})-\frac{Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\frac{Q}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}X_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})^{2}]}
converges to a constant so we leave it out.

Similar to
[
32
, Theorem 6.11]
, for
p>1
and
1/p+1/q=1
, we need to control two parts:

\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi R}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|F(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon})-F(\phi_{g}+u_{0})\right|^{p}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi R}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}\right|^{q}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c\right)^{1/q},

and

\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi R}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|F(\phi_{g}+u_{0})\right|^{p}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi R}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon},\partial\Sigma)}-e^{-\mu M_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\Sigma)-\mu_{\partial}L_{g}(\phi_{g}+u_{0},\partial\Sigma)}\right|^{q}\right]{\mathrm{d}}c\right)^{1/q}.

And their convergences are independent of
\omega
.

We only need to consider
F
of the form (
13
), then
\left|F(\phi_{g}+u_{\varepsilon})-F(\phi_{g}+u_{0})\right|
is determined up to some polynomial of
\langle u_{\varepsilon}-u_{0},f_{[\omega],1}\rangle,\cdots
. The second one is already proved in Lemma
7.3
.

Finally,

\sum_{[\omega]\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})}e^{-\pi R^{2}\int_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{reg}}|\omega|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}(\sum\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})+\sum\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})-QRK_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega))}

which converges absolutely by
2.8
.
∎

Suppose
F
has
pure degree

n
in the sense that
F(\phi+c)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}nc/R}F(\phi)
for
c\in{\mathbb{T}}_{R}
. Expanding the exponentials in the GMCs and collecting the terms involving the zero mode
c
, we see that the path integral has the form

\sum_{p,q\geq 0}\,a_{p,q}\exp\left({\mathrm{i}}\left(\frac{n}{R}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}-Q\chi(\Sigma)+p\beta+q\frac{\beta}{2}\right)c\right),

where the coefficients
a_{p,q}
do not depend on
c
. Integrating over
c
, the terms that do not vanish must satisfy the
neutrality condition

\frac{n}{R}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}-Q\chi(\Sigma)+p\beta+q\beta/2=0.

(17)

There are finitely many such terms. In particular,
\langle V_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}=0
unless

\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}\leq Q\chi(\Sigma).

This is analogous to the second Seiberg bound for real LCFT.

7.2
Segal’s amplitudes

Let
\Sigma
be an extended surface with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
and
g
a Neumann extendible conformal metric on
\Sigma
. Fix
{\mathbf{v}}
as usual. Similarly to the previous section, we define the following functionals on the Liouville field
\phi_{g}=[(X_{g},\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\overline{2\pi Rf}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)+c_{0})]
as in
3.9
.

•

For the curvature terms, we define

K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})=e^{{\mathrm{i}}Q\chi(\Sigma)c_{0}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}QRK_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)}\exp\left(\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}K_{g}(X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+2\pi Rf)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2\pi}\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}k_{g}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+2\pi Rf)\,{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}\right),

where
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is any separating family of
\Sigma
with respect to
{\mathbf{v}}
disjoint from
x_{0}
. If
QR\in 4{\mathbb{Z}}
(or
2{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\Sigma
has no corners), this is well-defined.

•

For the potential terms, we define

\displaystyle M_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})

\displaystyle=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta(c_{0}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\beta(P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+2\pi Rf)}M_{g}^{\beta}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}),

\displaystyle L_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})

\displaystyle=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(c_{0}+I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega))}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\beta}{2}(P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+2\pi Rf)}L_{g}^{\beta}(X_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}),

and

M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})=\mu\int_{\Sigma}M_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})+\int_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{N}}}\Sigma}\mu_{\partial}\,L_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g}).

If
\beta R\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}
(or
{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\mu_{\partial}=0
), this is well-defined.

•

For the electric operators, we define for
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(z_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}c_{0}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}2\pi Rf(z_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}X_{g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})},

\displaystyle V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{4}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(x_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}c_{0}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(x_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}2\pi Rf(x_{j})}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}X_{g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})},

and

V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})=\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j}).

The same remark applies concerning the well-definedness of this functional.

Definition 7.4
.

For a measurable function
F:{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the
BCILT amplitude
of
F
is the function
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F):{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}F(\phi_{g})\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}.

We define the following function class on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. The idea is that it should be a polynomial in
\phi_{g}
. Recall that we have a continuous surjection

\begin{array}[t]{ccccccc}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\times&{\mathcal{D}}_{0}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})&\times&C_{\mathbf{m}}^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})&\to&{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\\
X_{g}&&{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}&&\psi&\mapsto&[(X_{g},\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+\psi)]\end{array}

Fix a representative in each connected component of
C_{\mathbf{m}}^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
. If
\psi_{0}
is a representative, then any element
\psi
in its connected component
[\psi_{0}]
has the form
\psi_{0}+\overline{f}
where
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
is Neumann extendible and locally constant near
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma
. Here
f
is well-defined up to
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\overline{f}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}
can be regarded as an element in
{\mathbb{T}}_{R}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
be the linear space spanned by functions
F:{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
whose pullback via the surjection above has the form

F([(X_{g},\overline{P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}+(\psi_{0}+\overline{f}))])=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{{\mathbf{n}}}{R}\cdot\overline{f}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}}\,P(\langle X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+f,h_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}+f,h_{m}\rangle)\,G([\psi_{0}]),

(18)

where
\psi_{0}
is a chosen representative,
{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
P
is a complex polynomial,
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
with zero integral over
\Sigma
,
G:\pi_{0}(C_{\mathbf{m}}^{{\mathrm{M}},{\mathrm{const}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))\cong H^{1}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
is bounded. Here
\frac{{\mathbf{n}}}{R}\cdot\overline{f}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}}\overline{\frac{n_{j}}{R}f|_{c_{j}^{\mathrm{D}}}}
is well-defined up to
2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
,
X_{g}+f
is well-defined up to
2\pi R{\mathbb{Z}}
(recall that the equivalence relation on
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
allows only changing the representatives by a function vanishing on the Dirichlet boundary), so this is well-defined. It is easy to check that this function space does not depend on the choices of the representatives
\psi_{0}
. Note that
1\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Suppose
\forall\,j
,
\alpha_{j},\eta_{j}>Q
. For
F\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)
exists as a limit in
L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
,
where
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
is equipped with the measure defined in
Section

3.2
.

We postpone the proof to
Section

7.5
.

One should think of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)
as being antilinear (resp. linear) on the outgoing (resp. incoming) Dirichlet (semi)circles, so that it defines a bounded linear map
{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{-}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{+}^{\otimes{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}^{-}}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{+}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{+}^{\otimes{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}^{+}}
, where
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{D}}^{\pm}
,
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\mathrm{M_{D}}}^{\pm}
denote the number of outgoing or incoming circles or semicircles, respectively. See
[
30
, Section 7]
for a detailed discussion.

For an extended surface
\Sigma
with
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
, identifying functions
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R})=\{*\}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with points in
{\mathbb{C}}
, we define
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},*}(F)=\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}
, in accordance with the convention in
Section

4
for unifying the cases
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
. We view correlation functions as a special case of amplitudes in this sense. One should bear in mind, however, that the definitions are fundamentally different.

7.3
Weyl anomaly

Theorem 7.6

(
Weyl anomaly
)
.

For
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{R}})
Neumann extendible with
\rho|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
if
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
,
F\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\big(F\big(\cdot-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho\big)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\exp\left(\frac{c_{\mathrm{L}}}{96\pi}\Big(\int_{\Sigma}\big(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\Big)-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\Delta_{\alpha_{j},m_{j}}\rho(z_{j})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\Delta_{\eta_{j}}\rho(x_{j})\right),

where
c_{\mathrm{L}}
,
\Delta_{\alpha_{j},m_{j}}
,
\Delta_{\eta_{j}}
were defined in
1.2
.

Here
F(\cdot-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho)
is interpreted similarly to
3.4
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
F
is as in
Eq.

13
, then
F(\phi_{g}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}n(c-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}m_{g}(\rho))/R}\,P(\langle X_{g}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}(\rho-m_{g}(\rho)),h_{1}\rangle,\ldots\langle X_{g}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}(\rho-m_{g}(\rho)),h_{m}\rangle)\,G([\omega])
. If
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
and
F
is as in
Eq.

18
, then
F(\phi_{g}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{{\mathbf{n}}}{R}\cdot\overline{f}|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}}\,P(\langle X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho,h_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle X_{g}+P_{\Sigma}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho,h_{m}\rangle)\,G([\psi_{0}])
.

Proof.

Here is how each term changes under a change of the conformal metric:

•

The change of the measure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})
was calculated in
3.8
and
3.10
.

•

By (4) of
5.2
,

K_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})=K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})\,\exp\left(\frac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\langle d\rho,d\phi_{g}\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}\right).

•

We have
M_{e^{\rho}g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{e^{\rho}g})=e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}Q\rho}M_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}v_{g})
,
L_{e^{\rho}g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{e^{\rho}g})=e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}Q\rho}L_{g}^{\beta}(\phi_{g},{\mathrm{d}}\ell_{g})
, so formally

M_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})=M_{\Sigma,g}\big(\phi_{g}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho\big).

•

As
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle V_{\alpha_{j},e^{\rho}g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})

\displaystyle=V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(z_{j})\,e^{-\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{4}\rho(z)+o(1)},

\displaystyle V_{\eta_{j},e^{\rho}g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})

\displaystyle=V_{\eta_{j},g,\varepsilon}(x_{j})\,e^{-\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{8}\rho(z)+o(1)}.

First suppose either
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
\int_{\Sigma}\rho\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}=0
or
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\neq\varnothing
and
\rho|_{{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma}=0
. Omitting the integral sign
\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}
, the integrand is

\displaystyle F(\phi_{e^{\rho}g})\,V_{e^{\rho}g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{e^{\rho}g})\,K_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{e^{\rho}g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{e^{\rho}g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{e^{\rho}g}=F(\phi_{g})\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,K_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt\exp\left(\frac{1}{96\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\big(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{4}+\frac{m_{j}^{2}R^{2}}{4}\bigg)\rho(z_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{8}\rho(x_{j})+o(1)\right).

Applying the imaginary Girsanov transform to the GFF
X_{g}
with
Y=-\frac{Q}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\langle d\rho,dX_{g}\rangle_{g}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
,
{\mathbb{E}}[YX_{g}]=-\frac{Q}{2}\rho
,
{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{2}]=\frac{Q^{2}}{8\pi}\int_{\Sigma}|d\rho|_{g}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}
, we get

\displaystyle F(\phi_{g})\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,K_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,e^{\rho}g}(\phi_{g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}=F\big(\phi_{g}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}Q}{2}\rho\big)\,V_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})^{-1}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt\exp\left(-\frac{Q^{2}}{16\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\big(|d\rho|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\rho\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}v_{g}+Q\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}\rho(z_{j})+Q\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{4}\rho(x_{j})\right).

Summing up, we get the desired formula.

It remains to treat the case
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma=\varnothing
and
\rho
constant. Suppose
F
is as in
Eq.

13
. As discussed at the end of
Section

7.1
,
\langle FV_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{m}}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{\Sigma,g}=\sum_{p,q}a_{p,q}
where the sum is over
(p,q)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
satisfying the neutrality condition
Eq.

17
. Under the change from
g
to
e^{\rho}g
for
\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}
constant, the change of
a_{p,q}
is

\exp\left(-\left(p\beta+q\frac{\beta}{2}\right)\frac{Q}{2}\rho-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{j}^{2}}{4}+\frac{m_{j}^{2}R^{2}}{4}\right)\rho-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}^{2}}{8}\rho\right).

By the neutrality condition, this is equal to

\exp\left(\frac{n}{R}\frac{Q}{2}\rho-\chi(\Sigma)\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\rho-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}-Q\right)+\frac{m_{j}^{2}R^{2}}{4}\right)\rho-\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{j}}{4}\left(\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}-Q\right)\rho\right),

as desired.
∎

7.4
Spin

Theorem 7.7

(
Spin
)
.

For
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, we have

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)={\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}R\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha_{j}-Q)m_{j}\theta_{j}}.

Proof.

The terms that depend on
{\mathbf{v}}
are
K_{\Sigma,g,x_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\omega)
in
K_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g})
and
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}I_{x_{0}}(2\pi R\omega)(v_{j})}
in
V_{\alpha_{j},g,\varepsilon}(v_{j})
. The change of the former was calculated in
5.4
. The latter changes by
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{j}Rm_{j}\theta_{j}}
, as is easy to check.
∎

7.5
Gluing

Using the conventions and notations in
Section

4
, the gluing theorem for BCILT can be simply stated as

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma^{\#},g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{0}}(F^{\#},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}})=C\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}_{0}^{{\mathbf{k}}_{0}})\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k},

with
C
as in
4.1
, where
F^{\#}(\phi)=F(\phi|_{\Sigma})
. For the reader’s convenience, we spell this out.

Theorem 7.8

(
Gluing
)
.

We resume the notations in
Section

2.7
.

•

In the case of gluing two surfaces, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma\#\Sigma^{\prime},g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\times{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\times{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{v}}\times{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{x}}\times{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}}\times{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\mathrm{c}}\times{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\prime{\mathrm{c}}}}(F\#F^{\prime},({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})^{\mathrm{c}}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}})^{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle\qquad=C\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})^{\mathrm{c}})\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma^{\prime},g|_{\Sigma^{\prime}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\prime}}(F^{\prime},\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\prime{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}})^{\mathrm{c}})\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k},

where
(F\#F^{\prime})(\phi)=F(\phi|_{\Sigma})F^{\prime}(\phi|_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
, or more precisely,
(F\#F^{\prime})([(Y,\psi)])=F([(Y|_{\Sigma}-P_{\Sigma}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0}),\psi|_{\Sigma}+\overline{P_{\Sigma}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0})})])\,F^{\prime}([(Y|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}-P_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0}),\psi|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}+\overline{P_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0})})])
.

•

In the case of self-gluing a surface, we have

{\mathcal{A}}_{\#\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}}}(F^{\#},({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}})=C\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\zeta^{*}{\mathcal{C}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g|_{\Sigma},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times\widetilde{\varphi}^{k}\times({\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})^{{\mathrm{c}}{\mathrm{c}}})\,{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}^{k},

where
(\#F)(\phi)=F(\phi|_{\Sigma})
, or more precisely,
(\#F)([(Y,\psi)])=F([(Y|_{\Sigma}-P_{\Sigma}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0}),\psi|_{\Sigma}+\overline{P_{\Sigma}(\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\zeta^{*}Y|_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{0})})])
.

Here the constant
C
is as in
4.1
.

Here we assume that all functionals are in the function space
{\mathcal{E}}
, so that the amplitudes exist. It is straightforward to check that the function space
{\mathcal{E}}
is invariant under gluing.

Viewing amplitudes as operators, the case of gluing two surfaces amounts to composing their amplitudes, and the case of self-gluing a surface amounts to taking the trace of its amplitude.

Proof of
7.5
and
7.8
.

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}^{\varepsilon}(F)
the regularized amplitude before taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, whose existence can be proved using the
exponential moment of the Dirichlet GMC
. For a fixed
\varepsilon
, gluing for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon}
follows from
4.1
. Indeed, it suffices to check gluing for the functionals
K_{\Sigma,g}
,
e^{M_{\Sigma,g}}
,
V_{g,\varepsilon}
. Gluing for
K_{\Sigma,g}
follows from
5.3
. Gluing for the latter two is obvious.

We shall prove
7.5
by showing that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}^{\varepsilon}(F)\in L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))
for
\varepsilon>0
and it converges in
L^{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then
7.8
follows from gluing for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon}
by taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The idea is to use gluing to write the
L^{2}
inner product of amplitudes on
\Sigma
as a modified correlation function on the
Dirichlet double

\Sigma^{{\mathrm{D}}\#2}
, defined as the extended surface obtained by gluing
\Sigma
and its oppositely oriented copy
\overline{\Sigma}
along their Dirichlet boundaries via the identity map
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma\xleftrightarrow{\sim}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\overline{\Sigma}
on the Dirichlet boundary (compare
Section

2.1
). Clearly
\partial_{\mathrm{D}}\Sigma^{{\mathrm{D}}\#2}=\varnothing
. Again, we denote by
\tau
the
reflection
on
\Sigma^{{\mathrm{D}}\#2}
with respect to
{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma={\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\overline{\Sigma}
, so that
\overline{\Sigma}=\tau(\Sigma)
. By
7.6
, we may assume that the metric
g
on
\Sigma
is
Dirichlet extendible
in the sense that it doubles to a metric on
\Sigma^{{\mathrm{D}}\#2}
, denoted in the same way. For
F:{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}}}(\Sigma\setminus{\mathbf{z}},{\mathbb{T}}_{R})\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we have

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F,{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}})}=\int_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime{\mathrm{M}},{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}^{\mathbf{k}}}_{-{\mathbf{m}}}(\overline{\Sigma}\setminus\tau({\mathbf{z}})),{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}\overline{F(\phi_{g})}\,V^{-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},-{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})\,\overline{K_{\overline{\Sigma},g}^{-1}(\phi_{g})}\,e^{-\overline{M_{\overline{\Sigma},g}(\phi_{g})}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g},

where
\phi_{g}
is now the Liouville field on
\overline{\Sigma}
with respect to the metric
g|_{\overline{\Sigma}}=\tau_{*}(g|_{\Sigma})
, and we have made explicit the dependence of
V_{g,\varepsilon}
on the electric charges.
For
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, by
4.1
,

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F),{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=C\int_{{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbf{m}}\times(-{\mathbf{m}})}^{\prime{\mathrm{N}}}(\Sigma^{{\mathrm{D}}\#2}\setminus{\mathbf{z}}\cup\tau({\mathbf{z}}),{\mathbb{T}}_{R})}F(\phi_{g}|_{\Sigma})\overline{F(\phi_{g}|_{\overline{\Sigma}}\circ\tau)}\,V^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\times(-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}),{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\times(-{\boldsymbol{\eta}})}_{g,\varepsilon}(\phi_{g})

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023ptK_{\Sigma,g}^{-1}(\phi_{g}|_{\Sigma})\overline{K_{\Sigma,g}^{-1}(\phi_{g}|_{\Sigma})}\,e^{-M_{\Sigma,g}(\phi_{g}|_{\Sigma})-\overline{M_{\overline{\Sigma},g}(\phi_{g}|_{\overline{\Sigma}})}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{g},

where
C
is an absolute constant. While this is not a correlation function per se, one can show that this integral exists and converges as
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0
, similarly to the proof of
7.2
. Note that taking
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon
shows that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}^{\varepsilon}(F)
is
L^{2}
. Thus
\|{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)-{\mathcal{A}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{\Sigma,g,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{m}},{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(F)\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{*}{\widetilde{\partial}_{\mathrm{D}}}\Sigma,{\mathbb{T}}_{R}))}\to 0
as
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0
.

∎

8
Structure constants

An important step in the understanding of BCILT is the computation of its structure constants.

Suppose now all magnetic charges are zero. Our computations show that electric charges
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
must satisfy the neutrality condition

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{t}}\frac{\eta_{i}}{2}-Q\chi(\Sigma)+p\beta+q\frac{\beta}{2}=0,p,q\in\{0,1,2,\cdots\},

where
\chi(\Sigma)
is the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
. Hence, for a reasonable
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{s}}\alpha_{i}
, we have a finite number of
(p,q)
satisfying the neutrality condition. The correlation function can be written as the sum of several integrals

\begin{split}&\langle e^{{\mathrm{i}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\cdot\phi({\mathbf{z}})}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}{2}\cdot\phi({\mathbf{x}})}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{p\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{p+q}\mu^{p}}{p!q!}I(p,q,\mu_{\partial},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\mathbf{z}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{x}}).\end{split}

Now we consider a flat disk
\mathbb{D}
(
\chi(\mathbb{D})=1
) and
\mu_{\partial}
is a constant, then
I(p,q,\mu_{\partial},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\mathbf{z}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{x}})=\mu_{\partial}^{q}I(p,q,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\mathbf{z}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\mathbf{x}})
is proportional to

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{p}}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{q}}\exp&\bigg(2\pi(\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}G(z_{i},z_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\alpha_{i}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}G(z_{i},x_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\alpha_{i}\beta G(z_{i},w_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\alpha_{i}\frac{\beta}{2}G(z_{i},y_{j})\\
&\sum_{i<j}\frac{\eta_{i}}{2}\frac{\eta_{j}}{2}G(x_{i},x_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\frac{\eta_{i}}{2}\beta G(x_{i},w_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\frac{\eta_{i}}{2}\frac{\beta}{2}G(x_{i},y_{j})\\
&\sum_{i<j}\beta^{2}G(w_{i},w_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G(w_{i},y_{j})+\sum_{i<j}\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}G(y_{i},y_{j}))\bigg)\prod_{i}(1-|w_{i}|^{2})^{\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}y\ {\mathrm{d}}w,\end{split}

where
G(z,z^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|z-z^{\prime}||1-z\bar{z}^{\prime}|
is the Neumann Green function on
\mathbb{D}
. (In fact, the above integral is the expectation of “
p
-th moment of bulk GMC times
q
-th moment of boundary GMC” with Neumann GFF and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
insertions).

Suppose
\mu=0
, which forces
p=0
, and we only have one boundary insertion
\eta
at
x=1
. Then the above integral is proportional to

\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{q}}\prod_{k=1}^{q}|1-y_{k}|^{\eta\beta/2}\prod_{k<k^{\prime}}|y_{k}-y_{k^{\prime}}|^{\beta^{2}/2}{\mathrm{d}}y.

According to
[
24
, page 7, 1.17]
, it is equal to
M(\eta\beta/4,\eta\beta/4,\beta^{2}/4)
, which is

M(\eta\beta/4,\eta\beta/4,\beta^{2}/4)=\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\frac{\Gamma(1+\eta\beta/2+j\beta^{2}/4)\Gamma(1+(j+1)\beta^{2}/4)}{\Gamma(1+\eta\beta/4+j\beta^{2}/4)^{2}\Gamma(1+\beta^{2}/4)}.

When
\eta=0
, it becomes the Fyodorov–Bouchaud formula

\frac{\Gamma(1+q\beta^{2}/4)}{\Gamma(1+\beta^{2}/4)^{p}}.

Question 1:

When
\mu\not=0
is a constant, the easiest case is one
\alpha
insertion at
z=0
and one
\eta
at
x=1
. Then we need to consider all non-negative integers
p,q
s.t.
2p+q=2Q-2\alpha-\eta
, which is

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{p}}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{q}}&\prod_{j=1}^{p}|w_{j}|^{\alpha\beta}|1-w_{j}|^{\eta\beta}(1-|w_{j}|^{2})^{\beta^{2}/2}\prod_{j<j^{\prime}}|w_{j}-w_{j^{\prime}}|^{\beta^{2}}|1-w_{j}\bar{w}_{j^{\prime}}|^{\beta^{2}}\\
&\prod_{k=1}^{q}|1-y_{k}|^{\eta\beta/2}\prod_{k<k^{\prime}}|y_{k}-y_{k^{\prime}}|^{\beta^{2}/2}\prod_{j,k}|w_{j}-y_{k}|^{\beta^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}w.\end{split}

(19)

It seems like a combination of Dotsenko-Fateev and Selberg integrals, and to our knowledge, little literature studies this type of integral. There is a formula for the integral
[
21
, page 8, 5.6]
, but with only one insertion in the bulk (see our Question 2). Besides,
[
4
, Corollary 1.5]
provides a formula for only a bulk integral with term
(1-|w_{j}|^{2})^{\beta^{2}/2}
. Still, we wonder whether it would be possible to have a
formula
for (
19
).

Question 2:

Suppose we only have one insertion
\alpha
at
z=0
. Because we need
\alpha>Q
and
\alpha-Q+p\beta+q\beta/2=0
at the same time, then there is no non-negative
p,q
